   1|                                                                                 In Memoriam
   2|                                         Elisabeth "Sis" (Ryan) Montuori, 87, of Lunenburg, died peacefully July 28, 2020 surrounded by her
   3|                                         loving family. Her husband of 65 years, Lawrence "Lawrie" Montuori, died January 16, 2020. Sis worked
   4|                                         alongside Lawrie as bookkeeper of Lunenburg Oil Co, the business they operated together for 36 years. She
   5|                                         was a talented seamstress, working out of her home. Sis was a faithful member of St. Boniface Parish and St.
   6|                                         Boniface Women's Guild. For years, she oversaw St. Boniface's annual Red Cross Blood Mobile, earning
   7|                                         awards for the high volume of donations. She volunteered on the T.C. Passios Elementary School
   8|                                         Community Council, Lunenburg High School Football Boosters and the Council on Aging. Sis had a passion
   9|                                         and talent for cooking, interior decorating, entertaining, and gardening. She had an unconditional love of life,
  10|                                         family and friends. She leaves a son, three daughters, a half-brother, eight grandchildren, six great-
  11|                                         grandchildren and several nephews and nieces.
  12|                                          Bessie H. (Platt) Lindsay, 90, passed peacefully on July 30, 2020. Bessie was married
  13|                                          to the love of her life, Edmund K. Lindsay, who sadly left us in 2001. Bessie was born in Shandaken, NY,
  14|                                          daughter of the late William and Shirley Platt and one of seven siblings.
  15|                                          Bessie was proud of and dedicated to her community. Her volunteerism was extensive including at the Eagle
  16|                                          House and the Lunenburg Senior Center and the Lunenburg Friendly Seniors, where she was also a member.
  17|                                          Bessie was incredibly active and loved to have fun with family and friends!
  18|                                              Douglas K. MacMillan, 88, died August 7, 2020. He was the owner of Cherry Hill Dairy Farm. He and
  19|                                             his wife Marilyn celebrated their 64* anniversary on May 5th. He was born in Sweetsburg, Quebec on
  20|                                              November 22, 1931. He went to a one-room school in Bolton Pass until grade 6 and then attended
  21|                                              Knowlton Academy in Knowlton, Quebec. He moved to Lunenburg in 1951 with his parents and ran
  22|                                              Cherry Hill Dairy Farm, eventually becoming the owner. Douglas was a country music fan and he always
  23|                                              had the radio on and he could be heard singing all over the farm. He loved telling jokes, doing crossword
  24|                                              puzzles, playing cards and visiting family and friends. He was a member and a director of the
  25|                                            • Massachusetts Cooperative Milk Producers Federation, a longtime member and a director for 9 years of
  26|                                              ASCS, a lifetime member of The Grange and a member of the United Parish of Lunenburg.
  27|                                           Bruce W. Smith, 75, passed away August 26, 2020. He was a member of the first Lunenburg High
  28|                                           School football team and a 1963 graduate of Lunenburg High School. Bruce served in the U.S. Air Force
  29|                                           from 1963 to 1969 and then attended Northeastern University. He returned to the Lunenburg Football field
  30|                                          as an assistant coach from 1979 through 2004. Bruce was a Correctional Officer for the MA Dept. of
  31|                                           Corrections for 15 years. He enjoyed coaching the Lunenburg Blue Knights and acted as a father figure to so
  32|                                           many of his student athletes. Bruce had a positive impact on thousands of athletes which included many
  33|                                           enjoyable years with the Lunenburg Bengals as well as Lunenburg Youth football. Bruce was inducted into
  34|                                          the Massachusetts High School Football Hall of Fame as Assistant Coach of the Year in 2002 after 23 years
  35|                                           of dedication. Bruce loved spending time with his family and friends, and he lived life to the fullest. The
  36|                                           most important thing in his life was his family and he spoke about them often.
  37|                                            Donald F. Bowen, 88, passed away on October 6, 2020. Don was born in Boston on December 21,
  38|                                            1931. He attended high school in Boston and graduated from Northeastern University in 1962. Over his
  39|                                            career he worked in sales for a number of high-tech companies. He served in the U.S. Navy between 1950 -
  40|                                            1951. He moved to Lunenburg in 1980 and loved his home on Lake Shirley. He was active in his
  41|                                            community and served as a board member for the Lunenburg Zoning Board for 30 years, the latter years as
  42|                                            Chairman.
  43|                                            Don loved spending time with family and friends and he lived life to the fullest. He was a loyal and
  44|                                            generous friend and a loving and dedicated father. The most important thing in his life was his family.
  45|                                            He is survived by his daughter Dawn-Jean and son-in-law Drew; son Mark and daughter-in-law Donna; his
  46|                                            grandchildren, Justin, Nicole, Danielle, Anthony, Jacob and Alexandra and his great grandchildren, Liam,
  47|                                            Grace and Mae.
  48|                                                                                                2

===PAGE 5=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                                        LUNENBURG PROFILE
   2|                                                                       YEAR INCORPORATED- 1728
   3|                                                                        REGISTERED VOTERS-8628
   4|                                                                            FY'20 TAX RATE-$18.12
   5|                                  OPEN TOWN MEETING GOVERNMENT- FIVE MEMBER SELECT BOARD
   6|                                                    ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1S' SATURDAY IN MAY
   7|                                                   ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   8|                                                                        AREA- 26.63 SQUARE MILES
   9|                                                                          ROAD MILES- ABOUT 100
  10|              Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of approximately 26.4
  11|              square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts approximately 43 miles from Boston. It is
  12|              bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of
  13|              Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of
  14|              Townsend
  15|              Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered voters in the town.
  16|              Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of the town are generally administered
  17|              by a five member Select Board and a Town Manager. Local school affairs are administered by an elected
  18|              seven member School Committee while local taxes are assessed by a three member Board of Assessors, all
  19|              are appointed on an at-large basis for staggered three-year terms.
  20|              Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Three water bodies account for
  21|              somewhat of a clustered approach to the town's historical development.                                               Lake Whalom and the former
  22|              Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the remainder of Lunenburg. This district
  23|              was developed as a housing base for many factory workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster
  24|              and Fitchburg. In many ways the characteristics of the Whalom District closely reflect characteristics of
  25|              these cities more than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills area, around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  26|              developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. The third body of water is Shirley
  27|              Reservoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages that have now been modified to
  28|              year-round residences.
  29|              Commercial and industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major
  30|              arteries (Routes 2A and 13) and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of Leominster's
  31|              and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards had slowly developed into
  32|              single family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg. In 2013 the Annual Town Meeting voted to
  33|              establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve, and enhance farming in the community and to
  34|              administer the "Right to Farm" bylaw.
  35|              The 2015 Lunenburg Town Meeting established the Architectural Preservation District ("District") bylaw to
  36|              regulate construction, demolition or renovation of existing "Contributing Property" structures as defined by
  37|              the bylaw within an established bounded district. The bylaw was introduced at Town Meeting along with the
  38|              Village District Bylaw, which created an overlay zoning district to encourage mixed-use commercial and
  39|              residential development. While the Village District Bylaw encourages the economic vitalization of
  40|              downtown Lunenburg, the Architectural Preservation District Bylaw ensures that it will not come at the
  41|              expense of the town's historical character and integrity.

===PAGE 17=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                               REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   2|                                                                                 Fiscal Year 2020
   3|             This year, as I write this report on the achievements of fiscal year 2020, my mind immediately goes back to
   4|             March of 2020 when all our lives were turned upside down. We can all relate that this past year has been a
   5|             roller coaster and everyone has faced personal and professional challenges that no one could have foreseen.
   6|             From a local government standpoint, there were a myriad of obstacles and uncertainties facing cities and
   7|             towns across our nation that our local and school leadership team also faced, such as providing a continuity
   8|              of services to our residents and businesses during a pandemic, food insecurity for our youth and elders, re-
   9|             opening measures that adequately protected our employees, students and the public, remote learning for
  10|             students, remote public meetings, a vast array of changes in state and federal legislation that affected our
  11|             daily operations, and the list goes on. The town department heads and school leadership teams and our staff
  12|             came together during this time and met these challenges head on. As a town we witnessed attitudes of
  13|             perseverance, innovation, and an overall desire to help our residents, students, and businesses throughout
  14|             this pandemic. I want to recognize the immense thought and effort that town and school staff put into how
  15|             we provided services this past year and how everyone rose to the challenge.
  16|             During fiscal year 2020, in addition to all the responses that were needed in light of COVID-19, there were
  17|             many topics addressed and accomplishments to highlight:
  18|             • In July of 2019, as part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed their goals from FY19
  19|                   and formalized their goals for FY20. The goals the Board identified for FY2019 included identifying,
  20|                   defining, discussing, and finalizing the criteria to be used in determining the use and/or re-use of the
  21|                   Town Hall, Ritter, old Primary, Brooks House, and TC Passios and to propose at least one project to the
  22|                   2020 Annual Town Meeting regarding the use and/or reuse of the old Primary School. The Board
  23|                   identified the criteria used in determining the use and/or re-use of the aforementioned buildings in the
  24|                   fall of 2019 and discussed the future use of the old Primary throughout the year and continued to narrow
  25|                   down the focus for the future use of this building into 2020. The Board made progress on determining
  26|                   the future use of the old Primary School property as a future open space/passive recreation space with
  27|                   additional parking that is lacking in the center of town.
  28|             • The Town Manager's goals for FY 2020 included developing and implementing steps towards the
  29|                   redevelopment/reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, implementation of a recycling contamination
  30|                   enforcement program, and planning/implementing accessibility improvements. All of the action items
  31|                   identified for each of the Town Manager's goals were completed.
  32|             • In July and August the Board met multiple times to finalize a Special Event Policy. The Board also did
  33|                   their annual review of all their policies and adopted a new Buy Recycled Policy and made various
  34|                   updates to the existing policies. In September the Board adopted a Social Media Policy.
  35|             • This year was another productive year for infrastructure projects in Lunenburg. Besides the continuation
  36|                   of the $4M Pavement Management Plan on local roads, the Town continued to work with the
  37|                   engineering firm on the Pleasant Street Bridge Project that is expected to be complete by the spring of
  38|                   2022. The DPW also undertook culvert repairs on Northfield Road and Sunset Lane and repairs to the
  39|                   Townsend Harbor Bridge. The Summer Street Road Reconstruction project that was awarded in 2018
  40|                   was substantially complete by the fall of 2019.
  41|                                                                                               17

===PAGE 18=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|              • The Board and the Town Manager worked with Montachusett Planning Commission on a Walkability and
   2|                    Parking Study for the Main Street area and the Whalom area. A report was presented to the Board in
   3|                    December of 2019 and is available on the town website.
   4|              • In August of 2019 the Board created the TC Passios Building Design Committee that would work with a
   5|                    selected architectural/engineering firm on the schematic and design development of the space at the TC
   6|                    Passios building for town offices, school uses and community uses. The appointments for this Committee
   7|                    were finalized by the fall.
   8|              • In the fall of 2019 the Town Manager worked with a consultant to apply for grant funding through the
   9|                    Municipal Vulnerability Preparedness (MVP) Program, which provides support for cities and towns to
  10|                    plan for climate change and implement key adaptation actions for resiliency. The planning grant applied
  11|                    for is a necessary step in order to apply for construction costs to implement projects that are identified
  12|                    during the planning phase. The Town received a Municipal Vulnerability Preparedness (MVP) Planning
  13|                    Grant and hired a consultant to guide the planning process, including holding the required all day
  14|                    workshop, listening session, and to complete the vulnerability assessment and develop an action oriented
  15|                    climate resiliency plan. Communities who complete the MVP program become certified as an MVP
  16|                    community and are eligible for action grant funding to implement projects in their action plan. At the
  17|                    conclusion of this planning phase the Town submitted the action plan to the State, which was
  18|                    subsequently approved.
  19|              • The Town Manager presented a finalized Police Union agreement to the Board for a three year agreement
  20|                    in the fall that equated to a zero dollar impact to Fiscal Year 2020, mainly due to attrition in the Police
  21|                    Department during Fiscal Year 2020. The agreement included a 2.5% COLA for year one, 2% for year
  22|                    two, and 2% for year three and increased the educational incentive over three years. The costs for year
  23|                    two were estimated at $81,000 and year three costs were estimated at $74,000, with the total cost of the
  24|                    agreement to $139,000.
  25|              • The Special Town Meeting in November 2019 included a number of articles such as amending the FY
  26|                    2020 budget to capture additional tax growth, the purchase of the 994 Northfield Road (also called the
  27|                    Saliba property and/or Table Rock) for conservation and recreational purposes for $282,000, bylaw
  28|                    amendments, a non-binding question on the demolition of the Old Primary School property, acceptance of
  29|                    Butterfly Lane as a public way for municipal purposes, a deposit of $88,222 into the Special Purpose
  30|                    Stabilization Account and two home rule petitions for additional liquor licenses.
  31|              • In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler, weekday
  32|                    entertainment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses. Throughout the year, the
  33|                    Board reviewed Earth Removal permits, liquor license transfers, Special Event permit requests,
  34|                    flammable storage licenses, pole petitions, tollbooth requests, and held Dangerous Dog hearings.
  35|              • The Select Board held an Earth Removal Workshop in August 2019 and had the annual renewals for earth
  36|                    removal permits at the end of August. In January 2020, they held a public hearing for a new Earth
  37|                    Removal Permit at 1325 Massachusetts Avenue and approved the permit with a number of conditions.
  38|                    The Board held a discussion about the Earth Removal Permitting process at their February 18, 2020
  39|                    meeting.
  40|                                                                                               18

===PAGE 19=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|               • The Town Manager, DPW Director, and engineering firm WorldTech presented a proposal to the
   2|                    Selectmen for a Tier 3 Complete Streets application that the Board agreed to submit to MassDOT for
   3|                    Tier 3 funding totaling $258,000 in September of 2019. This application, which included improvements
   4|                    to the Main Street area, creation of an improved pedestrian crossing from the Ritter Building to Town
   5|                     Hall, sidewalk improvements to Memorial Drive, and improvements to the Main Street/Oak Avenue
   6|                    intersection. The Town received notification it was awarded $258,000 in construction funding in
   7|                    January of 2020. The engineers worked on the design plans throughout 2020.
   8|               • The Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2021 to the Finance Committee and
   9|                    Selectmen in mid-February. Part of the Town Manager's recommendation was to continue to fund the
  10|                    Other Post-Employment Benefits Trust Fund for the unfunded liability of retiree health insurance in the
  11|                    amount of $110,000, an additional $50,000 towards Pavement Management, and fund the regular
  12|                    Stabilization fund in the amount of $100,000. The FY2021 budget also included the hiring of an
  13|                    additional Full Time Patrolman, an additional Full Time Firefighter, additional hours for the Assistant
  14|                     Town Clerk and Board of Health Administrative Assistant, additional funding for Facilities and Public
  15|                     Rights of Way ADA Improvements, and a 2.5% increase for the Lunenburg Public Schools and Monty
  16|                    Tech. In mid-March department heads were still presenting their budgets to the Finance Committee and
  17|                    then the COVID-19 pandemic caused a ripple effect that meant revamping the entire FY 2021 budget.
  18|                     All new additions to the budget were put on hold in order to see the impact on our estimated revenues for
  19|                    FY21. The pandemic also required us to postpone the Annual Town Meeting from May until June and it
  20|                    was held on Saturday, June 13" with all public health and CDC safety protocols in place. The total
  21|                    omnibus budget for FY21 that was approved in June was $38,658,115. By the fall Special Town
  22|                    Meeting in November 2020, with the first quarter revenues trending in a normal trajectory, all the items
  23|                    that were deferred were able to be funded with the exception of the additional hours for the Assistant
  24|                    Town Clerk and the additional $50,000 for Pavement Management.
  25|                 There were multiple projects that addressed accessibility at various town facilities, town property, and
  26|                    public rights of way throughout the year. In February the engineering firm that completed the ADA
  27|                     Public Rights of Way Plan presented the results of the plan to the Select Board and recommendations for
  28|                    funding the non-compliant ramps, sidewalks, and crosswalks. The Town Manager applied for and
  29|                    received a District Local Technical Assistance grant to complete the ADA Self-Evaluation and
  30|                     Transition Plan by Montachusett Regional Planning Commission. The Town also received an earmark in
  31|                    FY20 for the repair of the handicapped ramp at the Town Hall in the amount of $27,000 that was put out
  32|                    to bid in April and was completed by the beginning of July.
  33|               • Various improvements were made to town facilities over the course of Fiscal Year 2020 including
  34|                    rebuilding the back entrance to the Ritter Building, repair of the DPW Building roof, upgrading the
  35|                    HVAC system in the older portion of the Eagle House Senior Community Center, energy efficiency
  36|                    improvements at the Public Safety Building, and repair of a portion of the Eagle House Senior
  37|                     Community Center roof. The removal of the underground fuel tanks located at the DPW and the
  38|                    installation of a new above ground tank commenced in September 2020.
  39|               • The redevelopment and/or reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, an old vacant gas station with potential
  40|                    environmental hazards, has been a property the Town has sought to clean-up for over a decade but due to
  41|                    the unknown environmental hazards have not taken the property for back taxes so that the onus of the
  42|                    potential clean-up costs was not borne by the Town. The Town Manager submitted a Brownfields Site
  43|                     Assessment Grant through Montachusett Regional Planning Agency for the costs of a Phase II
  44|                    environmental site assessment, which was approved in the amount of $30,000.
  45|                                                                                               19

===PAGE 20=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|               • The Town Manager applied for Community Compact funds to implement two best practices, a
   2|                     Continuity of Operations Plan and a Financial Trend Monitoring System. The State notified the Town
   3|                     Manager in May that both applications received funding, totaling $50,000. The pandemic highlighted
   4|                     the lack of a written Continuity of Operations Plan to ensure that there would be no interruption in
   5|                     services and back-ups for all personnel and programs were identified. Due to the uncertain economic
   6|                     times caused by the pandemic and the impact it had on the budget process, the implementation of a
   7|                     Financial Trend Monitoring System that would review certain key financial indicators and positive or
   8|                     negative trends was considered another important best practice. The consultant for both projects, the
   9|                     UMass Collins Center, began working with the Town in June. Both projects are expected to be complete
  10|                     by June of 2021.
  11|               • The Assistant Town Manager put out a bid for the disposition of town owned property at 17 and 23 West
  12|                     Street. There were no bids received in the initial bid process so it was re-advertised a second time and
  13|                     was awarded to the high bidder in the amount of $35,000.
  14|               • The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY 2021.
  15|               The most prominent event of 2020 was the issuance of a State of Emergency by Governor Baker on March
  16|               10, 2020 and the impact COVID-19 had on the operations of the town, the school, how it affected our
  17|               residents and the businesses in town. There were countless actions needed in response to the different
  18|               actions and COVID-19 related legislation that affected our day to day operations, but I will limit it to the
  19|               most significant undertakings:
  20|               • On March 10th            ', the day Governor Baker declared a State of Emergency in response to the outbreak of the
  21|                     Coronavirus, we were forced to take swift, decisive actions to ensure we could continue providing
  22|                     services to the public and our businesses in the event of a shutdown. On Tuesday, March 17"h the Town
  23|                     Manager, Chairman of the Select Board, Police Chief, Fire Chief, and Board of Health Agent provided a
  24|                     public update through Public Access on the actions that were being taken and public health information
  25|                     on the Coronavirus.
  26|               • Public meetings were suspended the week of March 16" and resumed the week of March 24" under the
  27|                     emergency changes made to the Open Meeting Law that allowed for fully remote meetings. The Town
  28|                     began using Zoom as our remote meeting platform, which meant a different method of scheduling all
  29|                     meetings, public access to be trained to broadcast Zoom meetings, and training for all board, committee,
  30|                     and staff members that would be using this platform.
  31|               • On March 24* the Select Board declared a local state of emergency and voted to delay the Annual Town
  32|                     Meeting from May until June 13" and on March 28'* the Board voted to file a Court Order to extend the
  33|                     deadline for nomination papers for the Annual Town Election. On March 31" the Board voted to
  34|                     postpone the Annual Town Election until June 20, 2020.
  35|               • Town Offices closed to the public on March 16" but the employees remained in the building. During this
  36|                     time an assessment was done to determine what steps were needed and what IT equipment was needed
  37|                     for employees to work remotely. Our IT staff did a phenomenal job getting everything in place for this
  38|                    to occur in short order. On March 23d non-public safety employees began working remotely and
  39|                     continued to do so until June 2nd
  40|               • In April, in response to the Federal Family First Coronavirus Response Act, the Town adopted an
  41|                     Emergency Family and Medical Leave Policy and an Emergency Paid Sick Leave Policy. The Board also
  42|                     voted to authorize emergency expenditures related to COVID-19 per MGL 44, Section 31 and voted to
  43|                     waive penalties and interest on tax bills that would be due on May 1" until June 29"
  44|                                                                                               20

===PAGE 21=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|              • The budget process was coming to a conclusion around the time the State of Emergency was issued and
   2|                   the unknown effect on Local Aid from the State and the impact on local receipts, required the Town
   3|                   Manager to revamp the entire budget. This revised budget cut all the new additions, deferred half the
   4|                   capital plan, and the deposit into the OPEB Trust Fund. The Town Manager also issued a spending freeze
   5|                   on non-essential hiring and expenditures in April in order to maintain a positive cash flow in the event of
   6|                   an impact to our local receipts in the fourth quarter of 2020.
   7|              • The passing of the Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and Economic Security Act (CARES Act) provided over $1
   8|                   Million in relief funding for unbudgeted, COVID-19 related expenses incurred after March 27, 2020 to
   9|                   the Town and other educational relief funds provided some relief to the Town and School for eligible
  10|                   expenses. CARES Act funds did not allow for revenue shortfalls but did pay for such things such as
  11|                   Personal Protective Equipment, IT equipment, plexiglass structures, additional cleaning needed at
  12|                   facilities and other COVID-19 related expenses.
  13|             • The Town participated in a regional Community Development Block Grant application that would
  14|                   provide microenterprise assistance for businesses and assistance to food banks. The application was
  15|                   approved in July of 2020.
  16|              • In June the State issued a temporary approval process for outdoor seating and outdoor liquor sales for
  17|                   licensed businesses per Executive Order #35. The Board approved all of the applications for outdoor
  18|                   seating and outdoor liquor sales from our local businesses.
  19|              • Since mid-March the Town Manager has provided weekly COVID-19 updates at the Select Board
  20|                   meetings with local, state, and federal updates. This information is posted to the town website at
  21|                   www.lunenburgma.gov/departments/town-manager/covid-19-information-center.
  22|              As of writing this report, it has been four years since I was appointed your Town Manager and it is a
  23|              responsibility that I take very seriously. The residents of Lunenburg demonstrate the true meaning of
  24|              community that I have witnessed in various acts, this was especially apparent in 2020. We thank all of our
  25|              community members who have helped their fellow residents throughout this pandemic.
  26|              I have the privilege of working with many talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents
  27|              and business owners of Lunenburg and are consistently looking for ways to better serve the public. The
  28|              accomplishments noted above, among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities, could not happen without
  29|              the cooperation of dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials that make the wheels of town government
  30|              turn. Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson and Assistant Town Manager Julie Belliveau for
  31|              all your hard work and keeping the office running smoothly. I would also like to thank the committee and
  32|              board members, department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town.
  33|              Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the current and former department heads that led our various
  34|              departments in 2020: James Marino, former Police Chief; Thomas Gammel, current Police Chief; Patrick
  35|              Sullivan, Fire Chief; Jack Rodriquenz, former DPW Director; Robert Oliva, current DPW Director; Karen
  36|              Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance Director; Myleen Mallari, Treasurer/Collector; Sue Byrne, Principal
  37|              Assessor; Steve Malandrinos, IT Director; Adam Burney, Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA Director;
  38|              Muir Haman, Library Director; Kathy Herrick, Town Clerk; and all their staff for their talent and working
  39|              together to make our town run as efficiently as possible. Lastly, thank you to the residents and businesses
  40|              that make Lunenburg a wonderful place to visit, work, and live.
  41|              Respectfully submitted,
  42|              Heather R. Lemieux, Town Manager
  43|                                                                                               21

   1|                                                                  TOWN SERVICES

===PAGE 52=== (ocr, 23 lines)
   1|                                           ARCHITECTURAL PRESERVATION DISTRICT COMMISSION
   2|              The year 2020 was a productive year for the Architectural Preservation District Commission (APDC)
   3|              even with the pandemic and the loss of some meeting time in the second part of the year.
   4|              The APDC:
   5|              • facilitated Applications for Certificate to Alter in order to renovate, construct or alter historic homes
   6|                   in the Architectural Preservation District (APD) and communicated with the public answering
   7|                   questions about rules and regulations in the APD,
   8|              • initiated a plan for the APDC to take an annual walk through the APD to assess the physical
   9|                   condition of the APD with the intent of keeping track of the district over time,
  10|              • advocated for the preservation of the old Primary School and the Brooks House
  11|              • designed, purchased, and placed signs indicating the Architectural Preservation District
  12|             • created a plan for the restoration of the Town Pound.
  13|              The commission received the resignation of Jim LaVeck who had been a member since its beginning. His
  14|               long hours of work over two years were instrumental in the formation of the APDC and its rules,
  15|               regulations, and procedures. He was an absolute joy to work with and made difficult work easier for all the
  16|               commission members in the formation of the APDC. His work along with the other members after the two
  17|               years of formation, proved that the APDC worked in its intention to protect the character and value of the
  18|               center of town for future generations. We are grateful for his service to Lunenburg.
  19|              The commission gained one new member, Tony Nicastro. Tony has a keen sense of history and will prove to
  20|               be an asset to the commission and to the town. The commission presently has 4 members.
  21|              Submitted by
  22|              Richard McGrath, Chairman
  23|                                                                                               52

===PAGE 53=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|                                                                         BUILDING DEPARTMENT
   2|              The Building Department is located in the Ritter Memorial Building located at 960 Massachusetts
   3|              Avenue.
   4|              The Building Department consists of the following personnel:
   5|              Building Commissioner/Zoning Officer-Casey Burlingame
   6|              Administrative Assistant to the Building Commissioner-Lisa Normandin
   7|              Electrical Inspector-Jack Biery
   8|              Plumbing and Gas Inspector-Gary Williams
   9|              During 2020 some unprecedented difficulties arose nationwide with the global pandemic occurring.
  10|              Despite this unfortunate time, the services performed by the Building Department did not change or
  11|              subside. The Building Department strives to ensure all work done in the Town of Lunenburg is
  12|              performed to state mandated regulations and codes, as well as local zoning by-laws. In addition to
  13|              Building, Plumbing, Gas, and Electrical permits, the
  14|              Building Department also oversees annual inspections for places of assembly, safety inspections for
  15|             liquor serving establishments and forms of intent required to issue business certificates from the Town
  16|              Clerk.
  17|             In fiscal year 2020 the following permits were issued:
  18|                   492 Building Permits
  19|                   322 ElectricalPermits
  20|                   158 Plumbing Permits
  21|             •     185 Gas Permits
  22|             • 1157 total issued
  23|              The state of Massachusetts is currently utilizing the 2015 International Building Code and 2015
  24|             International Residential Code, as amended by Massachusetts.
  25|              The Town continues to utilize ViewPoint online permitting software and all applications for work
  26|              requiring a permit can be done online. In addition, paper applications are available at the Building
  27|              Department office.
  28|              Submitted by
  29|              Casey Burlingame
  30|              Building Commissioner/Zoning Enforcement Officer

===PAGE 54=== (ocr, 21 lines)
   1|                                                                          CEMETERY COMMISSION
   2|               The Cemetery Commission and Superintendent Todd Holman met throughout the year to discuss cemetery
   3|               business. This year has seen several improvement projects undertaken. The trimming and shaping of the
   4|               historic hedges that border the entrances to both cemeteries has begun, with South Cemetery having been
   5|               completed. We hope to have the new building completed this coming year with the help of students and
   6|               instructors from Monty Tech. Also in the planning stages is the enlargement, redesign and paving of the
   7|               parking area at North Cemetery which will allow for year-round services at the columbarium and provide
   8|               ADA accessibility as well. The Goodrich family burial yard at the corner of Fish and Lincoln Street is also
   9|               scheduled to be restored and improved this coming year with help from the Historical Commission and
  10|               Chairman Richard McGrath. The Commissioners would like to thank the following people and businesses
  11|               whose help was invaluable this year:
  12|               • Robert Ullo for his assistance in plowing snow from the entrance to South Cemetery.
  13|               • Dave Passios for his volunteer efforts in keeping the well pumps in good working order.
  14|               • Leominster Monument Co. for their continued assistance whenever it is needed.
  15|               • Lunenburg Boy Scout Troop 1728 and Scout Michael Dumford whose Eagle Scout project was the
  16|                     straightening of over 100 headstones in the old section of South Cemetery.
  17|               The Cemetery Commission maintains an online mapping system for both North and South Cemeteries that
  18|               can be found at www.burialsearch.com
  19|               Respectfully Submitted,
  20|                                William Tyler                                    Lorraine Mendoza                                     Nancy Foss-Yasko

===PAGE 56=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                                                                            Conservation Commission
   2|              The Conservation Commission is a seven member commission, appointed to 3 year terms by the Select
   3|               Board, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL c. 40 § 8C. The Commission
   4|              enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL c. 131 § 40 and Chapter 239 of the local by-laws
   5|               for the protection of wetlands.
   6|              The year 2020 was as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and
   7|              meetings for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family
   8|              upkeep projects and larger scale industrial/commercial proposals despite the limitations set by the COVID-19
   9|              pandemic. The Conservation Commission's work continues into the year 2021 to work on clarifying the land
  10|              use policy to provide consistency with the original by-law and to find those sections that may be now out of
  11|              date and need to be modified. This effort is estimated to be completed by spring of 2021. The Conservation
  12|              Commission has implemented revisions to the local wetland regulations, which have been in effect for over
  13|              one year to date and have served the interests of protecting valuable community natural resources very fairly
  14|              and effectively.
  15|              The Commission also held numerous inspections with the commission administrator on weekends prior to
  16|              meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The
  17|              Commission continues to work with the town on familiarizing itself with the upcoming storm water mandates
  18|              from the EPA and working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codification as
  19|              well as aiding with the community implementations the "MS4" regulations.
  20|              The Conservation Commission continues to implement forest stewardship plans using grants as outlined in
  21|              the last two annual reports. The stewardship plan for the Hollis Road property is oriented toward enhancing
  22|              bird wildlife habitat in conjunction with the Mass Audubon Society as part of the DCR "Forestry for the
  23|              Birds" program. The forest stewardship plans provide environmental management plans which include
  24|              timber-stand management. The Commission retained Gary Gouldrup of New England Forestry Consultants
  25|              who is a noted forester with years of experience working in Lunenburg and the adjacent towns/cities serving
  26|              municipalities and private landowners. The Commission continues to hold public forums on the
  27|              implementation of its open space policies and timber-stand management. The Commission expects to
  28|              continue to apply for and receive funding from the Commonwealth to author stewardship plans for our
  29|               Conservation Areas.
  30|              The Conservation Commission also has commenced planning for the forest management of the RAYNO
  31|               property and worked with the Upen Space Committee to complete the acquisition of the Saliba Property on
  32|               Howard Street adding signiticant acreage of open space. Copies of the current environmental stewardship
  33|               plans are available on the Conservation Commission website at:
  34|              https://www.lunenburgma.gov/files-docs/18-05-21/forest-stewardship-plans
  35|              The Commission was awarded a Recreational Trails Program grant to provide color coded trail markers and
  36|              coordinated "You Are Here" trail map signs to expand the safe use of Lunenburg's existing trail network.
  37|              Regional trail system map signs would show how trails are interconnected among multiple properties. The
  38|              project will also provide informational kiosks and emergency access gates. During 2020 trail maps were
  39|               developed for eleven conservation properties. These trail maps will be posted on a Conservation Commission
  40|              Trails website, on informational kiosks on the properties and on "You Are Here" trail map signs at trail heads
  41|               and trail intersections.
  42|              The Commission has also continued to work closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills on the
  43|              implementations of their lake management plans. These plans set goals and action items designed to give a
  44|              better picture of the overall health of the lakes and define various strategies to ensure continued protection of
  45|               these valuable resources.
  46|              The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and looks forward to many more!
  47|              Respectfully Submitted,
  48|              Bob Pease, Chair, Kenneth Jones, Vice-Chair, Jeffrey Viviano, Catie Childs, Carl Luck, Jack Rabbitt
  49|              Michael Larouche, Associate Member; Matthew S. Marro, Conservation Administrator
  50|                                                                                               56

===PAGE 57=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|                                                                              COUNCIL ON AGING
   2|                                                                                            FY20
   3|                                                                          July 1, 2019 - June 30, 2020
   4|             The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   5|             supportive services, programs and education. Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts
   6|             General Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or
   7|             services and to develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.
   8|             The FY20 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative
   9|             Assistant and Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Meal Site Coordinator; Judy
  10|             Alario and Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants; Jim McGuigan, Paul Grunditz, and David Gallagher, MART
  11|             Van Drivers.
  12|             Sadly, Judy Alario passed away in November 2019 and Paul Grunditz resigned in May 2020.
  13|             The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Lunenburg Select
  14|             Board. The FY20 Council on Aging Board members were; Chairperson Deb Lincoln, Vice-Chairperson Jane
  15|             Rabbitt, Barbara Brown, Betty DiGiacomo, Sarah Grant, Pete Lincoln, Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, John
  16|             Pesenti, Susan O'Sullivan and Judith Tarbell.
  17|             The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House
  18|             Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts,
  19|             and Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.
  20|             On March 13, 2020 the Eagle House Senior Community Center closed to the public due to COVID-19
  21|             restrictions. Staff members worked delivering Meals on Wheels from the senior center and making wellness
  22|             calls from home. Van service was suspended in March and resumed again in May 2020. Outdoor programs
  23|             such as "Drive Through" lunches were added in June 2020 and specific "indoor" programming resumed with
  24|             COVID-19 restrictions in place in mid-July 2020.
  25|             We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House. In FY20 we had 70 volunteers who
  26|             contributed 1,743.25 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the Meals on Wheels
  27|             program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Cafe, office help, library, and friendly visitors. We
  28|             greatly appreciate their service.
  29|             The Eagle House offers a variety of programs such as, Meals on Wheels, transportation, health and wellness,
  30|             arts and crafts, intergenerational, bocce, shuffle board, cards and games, cultural, information and referral,
  31|             Memory Café, COVID-19 Grab & Go programs, health insurance counseling and social events both at the
  32|             senior center and off-site to people age 50+. There were approximately 1031 people who utilized the senior
  33|             center in FY20. Those 1031 people compiled 22,946 units of duplicated service by participating in the
  34|             programs listed above.
  35|                                                                                               57

===PAGE 58=== (ocr, 20 lines)
   1|                                                                               COUNCIL ON AGING
   2|                                                                                             PY20
   3|                                                                           July 1, 2019 - June 30, 2020
   4|               The Lunenburg Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you online at
   5|               www.ourseniorcenter.com or you can pick up a copy at the Eagle House Senior Community Center, Town
   6|               Hall, Library, Post Office, local churches and various businesses throughout the community.
   7|               We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance
   8|               of supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders.
   9|               Special thanks go to our 501c3 fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc. This group provides funding
  10|               for new living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, and garden upkeep
  11|               at the Eagle House Senior Community Center. Also special thanks to all of our outside agencies for their
  12|               time and funding for support of programming for our clients.
  13|               In closing, I want to recognize the staff and volunteers at the Eagle House Senior Community Center for
  14|               their unwavering commitment to our Lunenburg elders. The COVID-19 pandemic forced us to make
  15|               immediate changes that significantly changed the lives of our elders during the pandemic. Our staff and
  16|               volunteers are to be commended on their dedication, flexibility and strength that they have shown throughout

===PAGE 59=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                                                 DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|              The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Parks, Cemetery, Sewer, Facilities
   3|              Departments, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel and
   4|              equipment to best serve the residents of the community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope of
   5|              services with a high degree of efficiency.
   6|                    "The mission of the Department of Public Works is to protect, preserve, and improve the Town's
   7|                   infrastructure and related assets in a manner which meets and exceeds the current and future social
   8|                   and economic needs of the community, to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality environment for the
   9|                    lown's residents and visitors, and to provide a cost effective and environmentally sound means of
  10|                    alsposing of solid waste and wastewater. "
  11|              DEPARTMENT PHILOSOPHY
  12|              Honesty and integrity are absolutes. They characterize the only forum in which anything worthwhile can be
  13|              accomplished.
  14|              Efficiency: Make every effort to proceed correctly the first time. It may take a bit longer but will not require
  15|             repeated attempts.
  16|              Loyalty up and down the chain of command is essential to success.
  17|              Every employee is essential. Those at the top are support people. The real work is done by the men and
  18|              women who drive the trucks, operate the equipment, turn the wrenches, and provide a seamless flow of
  19|              paperwork.
  20|              DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
  21|              Public Works Staff is either working or on call twenty-four hours a day three hundred and sixty-five days a
  22|              year prepared to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility related emergency that may arise.
  23|              We strive to enhance the awareness of the value Public Works provides to our Town, and ensure others are
  24|              aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to make sure efforts are not
  25|              dismissed as they merge into the fabric of our everyday expectation of service.
  26|              Major events, particularly in the form of devastating storms, over the past years have highlighted the fact that
  27|              Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency management as first responders.
  28|              Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is critical in order to facilitate the required
  29|              services of others. We must continue to build on this awareness.
  30|              This Department must reach out to the public we serve, continuing to make them aware of the fact that the
  31|              common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated staff of individuals,
  32|              service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid waste collection and recycling
  33|              operations, tree removal, storm water management, grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal,
  34|              cemeteries and parks are necessary services supporting everyone in the community. We will continue to
  35|              engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather support for annual budget requests,
  36|              capital requests, and specific projects.
  37|              DEPARTMENTAL GOALS FOR FISCAL 2021
  38|             • Continue to coordinate administrative directives with the activities of all public service departments and
  39|                   organizations within the DPW to improve service levels. Strive always for a higher level of efficiency.
  40|              • Coordinate with those who work daily in Town facilities to best prioritize capital improvements. DPW
  41|                    plays a major role in the enhancement of the work environment of all Town employees.
  42|              • Increase involvement with our insurance provider to make better use of available resources in an effort to
  43|                   obtain credits toward lessening premiums. Safety is our first concern.
  44|              • Encourage and facilitate DPW personnel to obtain and maintain required licensure in order to provide an
  45|                   optimal level of service.
  46|                                                                                               59

  10|             PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  11|             It had become a high priority goal of the Board of Selectmen as Road Commissioners, to achieve a pavement
  12|             condition of 72%-75% Town-wide. While State supported Chapter 90 funds remain stagnant, the dedicated
  13|              Pavement Management line in the operational budget has grown substantially. Additionally, with the infusion
  14|             of funds facilitated by a four million dollar debt exclusion supported by residents, the Comprehensive
  15|             Pavement Management Plan has been hugely successful providing smooth and safe transportation for our
  16|             residents and all those travelling through our community.
  17|             The debt exclusion funding combined with state and operational funds has nearly tripled the total paving value
  18|             of years past as we attack a nine-million-dollar backlog of repairs.
  19|             Time spent addressing pot holes and other roadway deficiencies diminish exponentially as new paving
  20|             increases. A clear benefit of the dollars expended. There still remains work to do, but expectations are high.
  21|             Funding for the reconstruction of the Pleasant Street Bridge was obtained through the State's Small Bridge
  22|             Program. Working with our engineering consultant VHB and Contractor R. Bates & Son, construction is
  23|             expected to commence in spring of 2021. Work also continues on the Complete Streets Design to enhance
  24|             walkability in the Town Center, with the expectation of the construction work being put out to bid in spring of
  25|             2021.
  26|             Acquiring supplemental funding allows the completion of projects that would otherwise not be viable due to
  27|             the extraordinary expense.
  28|             FLEET MAINTENANCE
  29|             The DPW Maintenance Division is responsible for the repair and preventative maintenance to all Town
  30|             vehicles and equipment and is carried out by a single, skilled and dedicated fleet Mechanic with support by
  31|              Department personnel. High performance police units, first responder Fire and Rescue apparatus, park,
  32|             cemetery, and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by the
  33|             Maintenance Division. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more frequent and therefore more
  34|             costly. Due to the volatile global market, the overall cost of fuel and petroleum based products continues to be
  35|              a moving target and therefore difficult to estimate annually.
  36|              Use of a fleet maintenance software program continues to be a valuable tool. Each repair is logged by
  37|             department and vehicle identification number and stored in a comprehensive database. Every vehicle can be
  38|             referenced for part numbers, required services, as well and repair costs over the life of the equipment.
  39|              Preventative maintenance continues to be a most valuable component of the program. Work will continue to
  40|             update and evaluate a vehicle replacement schedule based upon American Public Works Association
  41|             recommendations in order to more clearly represent the capital needs of the Department.
  42|             FACILITIES MANAGEMENT

  44|                              DPW Facility                                                  Public Safety Complex

   3|                               The Eagle House                                               Public Library
   4|                               All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding the school campus
   5|              A facilities staff of one skilled, dedicated individual remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year to
   6|              respond to alarm calls and emergencies at all public buildings within the Department's care.
   7|              Collectively the Department attempts to keep all buildings compliant with current codes to insure a safe
   8|              secure work environment. Any significant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential
   9|              of disrupting or disabling necessary services provided to the community. The Facilities Director is a problem
  10|              solver and technician with a wide range of experience encompassing all of the construction trades. Together
  11|              with a stable of reliable Contractors, the Department delivers services directly or oversees those functions
  12|              provided by specialized Contractors.
  13|              Moving forward the five year capital plan for facilities in 2021, as presented by the Department, will focus on
  14|             the needs of the public building envelopes utilizing the reports prepared by Vertex as a guide. Protecting and
  15|              maintaining the Town building exteriors is a priority even with the uncertain futures of some of the buildings.
  16|              This Department is keenly interested in the proposed T.C. Passios building reuse plan and how it impacts the
  17|              future use of Town Hall and the Ritter Building. A clearly defined plan for the future use of all buildings
  18|              would facilitate planning of capital expenditures.
  19|              ENVIRONMENTAL
  20|              Storm Water
  21|              As a component of the aforementioned "debt exclusion" approximately five hundred thousand dollars of the
  22|              four million in total has been earmarked for storm drainage including but not limited to repair of the existing
  23|              collection system, and new construction. We have already exceeded this amount and continue to perform
  24|              necessary repairs to the town-wide drainage system.
  25|              Before roads are paved, sub surface drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed to avoid paving
  26|              over problems. In the future new Federal and State storm water regulations will likely force the Town to
  27|              become more proactive albeit with no additional identified source of funding.
  28|              At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall drainage structures in close proximity to open water are
  29|              cleaned and inspected. Public Works maintains an aggressive schedule of street sweeping and catch basin
  30|              cleaning, the front line of storm water management.
  31|              Wastewater
  32|              Working with the Sewer Commission, the DPW continues to be responsible for the day to day operation and
  33|              maintenance of the wastewater collection system. Maintenance of the collection system, inclusive of 10 pump
  34|              stations, is outsourced to a specialty vendor but continues to be the responsibility of the DPW. Business
  35|              management, inspections, and permitting remain in-house and the responsibility of the DPW.
  36|              Tree Removal
  37|              Storm damage together with the natural attrition creates a backlog of tree felling and pruning that typically
  38|              exceeds the Department's allotted budget for tree removal. The Department strives to fulfill each resident
  39|              request but must prioritize work based upon the perceived degree of danger to residents and property. With
  40|              the establishment of a Hazardous Tree Survey, a more efficient approach to defects has been established
  41|              through prioritization. Currently, the amount of work far exceeds the funding available for it. As this is the
  42|              case, the DPW will continue to act in a reactionary manner as needs arise. All work aside from brush
  43|              removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees will continue to be outsourced due to the necessity
  44|              of a bucket truck or crane and a licensed professional. Each spring DPW staff works to remove and dispose of
  45|              woody debris within the Town right-of-way along with a Town-wide program of selective curbside mowing
  46|              and vegetation clearing. An aggressive program by Unitil in recent years has lessened the burden of the Town
  47|              by removing those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.
  48|                                                                                               61

===PAGE 62=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                                                                  DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|              Landfill
   3|              The capped, closed landfill off Young's Road remains in compliance with the inspection protocol mandated
   4|              by the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive methane gas as
   5|              well as groundwater contamination.
   6|              The frequency of the testing reduced through a special permit, as a result of consistently favorable results,
   7|              remained constant throughout the year. The landfill site including the perimeter fencing is inspected on a
   8|              regular schedule by DPW personnel.
   9|              Adjacent to the close landfill is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined
  10|              by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are allowed to store these materials through the procurement of a
  11|              (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of these materials. Other
  12|              communities must transport these tainted materials to an appropriate site at a substantial cost to the
  13|              community.
  14|              On weekends during the fall and spring, DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual composting of brush,
  15|              leaves, and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and our overall recycling
  16|              program. There has to date been no dedicated funding toward the organization or long-range remediation of
  17|              this collection area. With public water and sewer at close proximity, this acreage has been identified as a
  18|              potential recreational area for the Town.
  19|              Solid Waste
  20|              In the past, Public Works would have the responsibility to monitor, order, store, and distribute trash bags in
  21|              order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and recyclables by our current vendor, Casella Inc. under the
  22|              Pay as You Throw (PAYT) program. Our supplier continues to coordinate the effort between supplier and
  23|              vendor. DPW remains the point of contact between residents and the vendor responsible for curbside pick-up.
  24|              The current contract with Casella will come to an end at the end of fiscal year 2021 and this service will have
  25|              to go out to bid.
  26|              Lake Shirley Dam
  27|              In the early 1990's with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding, The Lake Shirley Association turned over
  28|              ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for
  29|              maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed by a small number of volunteers from the Lake
  30|              Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the
  31|              establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella of
  32|              the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates,
  33|              compliance with current and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a
  34|              professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a
  35|              positive report and we remain in total compliance.
  36|              Winter Operations
  37|              Snow and ice present a major challenge. The safety of the public depends on the ability of the DPW to
  38|              maintain our roadways at a high level of safety despite the severity or the duration of snow and icing events.
  39|              To that end, we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed plan with flexibility to
  40|              accommodate the unpredictability of each storm. The expectation of immediate service is high and must be
  41|              delivered to every part of Town in a timely manner.
  42|              Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the capital plan. Financial support is
  43|              necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of service due to breakdowns and
  44|              repairs. In the fall, salt is delivered to the DPW facility. Lunenburg is in a consortium with 14 other
  45|              communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price. From late summer to early fall vehicle
  46|              maintenance staff inspects and prepares the trucks, spreaders, and plows for winter operations. During the
  47|              construction season road crews address right-of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in
  48|              order to eliminate obstacles to snow removal. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations.
  49|                                                                                               62

===PAGE 63=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                                                  DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|              Generally, it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event, that determines the overall cost. For
   3|              example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a long duration freezing rain
   4|              event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically, and as allowed by State law, the Departments
   5|              snow removal accounts operate in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of snow removal
   6|               operations for an average winter.
   7|              The Town and its residents are fortunate to have a dedicated group of DPW personnel and private Contractors
   8|              who tirelessly and successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer regardless of severity or duration.
   9|                                                           "DPW workers are the original Storm Troopers"
  10|              PARKS
  11|              In conjunction with the five elected Park Commissioners, DPW staff oversees and maintains all parks and
  12|              grounds exclusive of school property. While resources of the DPW are available as required, much of the
  13|              Town-wide landscape maintenance is outsourced, inclusive of the playing fields, resulting in significant
  14|              savings and increased efficiency over an in-house operation. Marshall Park accommodates the High School
  15|              baseball and softball schedules, Little League, and the Lunenburg Phillies. McNally and Fitzgerald Field
  16|              provide for an active youth program as well. Commissioners have initiated a fee schedule at the fields and
  17|              the Town Beach. The Town Beach is staffed with employees of the Parks and Recreation Department during
  18|              the beach season. Wallis Park was the host of the Spring and Summer Basketball clinics through the
  19|              Recreation Department. The Parks and Recreation Department started year round programs for youth and
  20|              teens. Over the next year the Parks and Recreation Department plans to continue adding on programs for all
  21|              ages, summer programs and camps, family and community events as well.
  22|              CEMETERY
  23|              One dedicated Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and
  24|              South Cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three
  25|              members of the Cemetery Commission. The DPW supplies equipment and support staff for, on average,
  26|              forty burials a year and general maintenance of the facilities as required by Town Charter.
  27|              At the time this Town Report is being drafted, it marks the first time in many years that the Lunenburg DPW
  28|              is not being guided by Jack Rodriquenz. As the new DPW Director taking his place, and on behalf of the
  29|              entire Department, I would like to take the opportunity to thank Jack for his many years of dedicated service
  30|              to Lunenburg and wish him all the best in retirement.
  31|               As the new Director I look forward to a long and productive tenure serving the Town and its residents. I hope
  32|              to form many strong relationships with the Town's administrative body and residents as well as the many
  33|              Boards, Commissions, and Committees that help it all function. It is my goal to provide the highest levels of
  34|              service, that Lunenburg's residents expect and deserve, among all the divisions that make up the Department
  35|              of Public Works. I'm thankful and excited to have been given this opportunity and will serve the Town to the
  36|              best of my abilities.
  37|               As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure, our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the Town of
  38|               Lunenburg for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associated with
  39|              the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella of public works.
  40|              Respectfully,
  41|              Robert E. Oliva
  42|              DPW Director
