===PAGE 64=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|                                                                   FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   2|                                                                                    Fiscal Year 2021
   3|              The Annual Town meeting is an important event for community members to come together and vote on
   4|             warrants that directly impact how we manage our Town. Under this umbrella is the town budget. Throughout
   5|             the year the Town Manager and Department Heads carefully review expenses and have thoughtful
   6|             conversations about how to best serve the town. While developing the budget they considered funding,
   7|             contracts, and mandates, as well as what the community wants and needs to thrive. A target budget was
   8|              finalized and presentations were in the process of being made by the Town Manager and Department Heads
   9|             through several Finance Committee forums. The process came to a halt when we were struck by the
  10|              Coronavirus Pandemic. Suddenly, people were staying at home and being out of work. This was followed by
  11|             an immediate reduction in normal tax revenue (which continues to dwindle). This means our projections for
  12|              State Aid, local receipts, and other revenue have been decreased (down $300,290 from the pre-COVID19
  13|             budget). To account for this shortfall, and anticipate future revenue shortfalls, the budgets of several
  14|             departments and plans were reduced to leave a $1,055,950 surplus. Even with this, the budget maintains level
  15|             service funding for all departments. Some significant reductions from the target budget are:
  16|                    Lunenburg Public Schools                          $192,157
  17|                    Capital Plan                                      $664,333
  18|                    DPW                                               $102,000
  19|                    Additional Firefighter                            $95,359
  20|                    Additional Police Officer                         $84,664
  21|             It is important to note the whole budget will get a fresh review for the fall Special Town Meeting. By that
  22|             time, clearer revenue projections should be available. It could mean that more revenue will come in, or it
  23|             could mean that we need to use the surplus to stay afloat. While we need to implement a budget now, but there
  24|             will be opportunities to make changes to the budget in the future. The total requested budget appropriation is
  25|             $38,658,115. Armed with all that information, the Finance Committee recommends the budget as put forth by
  26|             the Town Manager. It is clearly acknowledging that the budget does the best is can with the resources
  27|             available. The budget, as a whole, is conservative, relying on only available tax levy and state aid for
  28|             operating and capital expenses. It also makes deposits into our stabilization account to allow for future use
  29|             towards unanticipated expenses or to be available for other funding needs that could arise due to COVID19.
  30|              The OPEB allocation, the deposit we make into a fund to pay for future retiree benefits, is also being re-
  31|             allocated to the stabilization fund. Each department also played a crucial role in making the budget work. As
  32|              was seen during the budget presentations, all departments follow a sincere mission to serve our community.
  33|              They have goals and initiatives to constantly improve their services to the town. It is with a heavy heart that
  34|             some of the work toward these goals will have to be put on hold. We are grateful for the grace with which
  35|              departments responded to the impacts of the Coronavirus Pandemic.
  36|              While the budget ultimately boils down to dollars and cents, there are factors not easily captured by money but
  37|             are worth mentioning as a way to look holistically at the financial picture of our Town, not just as a response
  38|             to COVID19.
  39|             • The largest part of the Town budget is the school budget. It is 57% of the Town's expenses, at
  40|                   $22,029,972 (including Monty Tech assessment). Additionally, the Town budget includes $1,076,000 in
  41|                   other school-related costs such as School Retiree Health Insurance. This is all significant dollars but still
  42|                   falls short of what is proposed by the school department for best serving our students. Projected School
  43|                   State Aid (Chapter 70) funding changes resulting from the Student Opportunity Act have a projected
  44|                   negative impact on Lunenburg. In fact, state aid increased at a smaller rate (down 1.1% or $78,483 less
  45|                                                                                               64

===PAGE 65=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                                                                   FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   2|               than last year) while the amount Lunenburg has to pay both our school system and Monty Tech
   3|               increased at a higher rate (5.85% or $356,763 and 0.92% or $88,131, respectively). The state's recalculation
   4|               of Chapter 70 aid was primarily aimed at high poverty districts and we did not qualify. Without increased
   5|               Chapter 70 funding the school budget is constrained by the tax levy.
   6|               • The capital plan was cut in half to contribute to the surplus necessary to prepare for what may lie ahead
   7|                    in revenue shortfalls. What is being proposed is being funded entirely by free cash with no additional
   8|                    funds from tax levy. We all agree on the importance of maintaining Town assets and keeping ahead of
   9|                    obsolescence and disrepair. The recommendations are based on the Capital Planning Committee's
  10|                    prioritization of the requests approved. The current budget funds 12 of those recommendations. More
  11|                    may be funded if there is revenue to do so in the fall. Please note the Finance Committee deferred on
  12|                    making a recommendation on the Capital Plan until the annual Town Meeting to allow for final revenue
  13|                     adjustments.
  14|              • New Growth revenue for Lunenburg is trending down. The five-year financial forecast posted a new
  15|                     growth estimate for 2021 at $173,400. The proposed budget is conservative and can absorb the potential
  16|                    revenue loss. However, without significant housing projects (like Tri-Town and Emerald Place) new
  17|                    growth figures are not expected to reach prior levels within the next three years.
  18|               • Some of our departments fund regular, ongoing expenses with donations and grants. Those funding
  19|                    sources are not guaranteed but the services they fund are vital. To ensure those services are dependably
  20|                    funded, the Town has taken on some facilities expenses for the Library and some kitchen staff personnel
  21|                    expenses for the Council on Aging as part of the regular Town budget.
  22|               • Lunenburg's debt ratio has been evaluated as "poor" by an independent agency. There will be a
  23|                    significant drop in debt service as we pay off the Public Safety Building and Primary School loans in
  24|                    2024. However, it is likely we will take on more debt to cover future needs, such as what may be
  25|                    necessary for Turkey Hill Elementary School. There are no easy answers for debt management. The
  26|                    Finance Committee will be putting together a Debt Project group with the goal of better understanding
  27|                    the issue and its ramifications to the Town.
  28|               The F21 budget is not easy for anyone to accept. It is understood that it does not meet the financial policies
  29|               of the Town (re-allocated OPEB) nor does it meet the needs of the Town on an ongoing basis. Right now it
  30|               is the best that can be done under the circumstances we are in. For that reason, the Finance Committee is
  31|               supporting the budget. Because the budget is conservative the Town will be in a better position to navigate
  32|               uncertain waters that may lie ahead in FY21 and FY22 as COVID-19 unfolds further. We know Lunenburg
  33|               will rise to the challenges of the years to come. This year's budget keeps us moving in the right direction.
  34|               The Finance Committee thanks the Town Manager and all Department Heads for their exemplary service to
  35|               the citizens of Lunenburg. They demonstrate genuine caring for our community and the role they have in
  36|               shaping the quality of our lives every day.
  37|               Finance Committee

===PAGE 66=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                                                                       HISTORICAL COMMISSION
   2|             The year 2020 was a productive year for the Lunenburg Historical Commission. The Commission
   3|             accomplished a lot in spite of the COVID-19 pandemic.
   4|             The Commission:
   5|             • facilitated demolition delay applications and answered questions from the public concerning historic
   6|                  properties,
   7|             • joined in partnership with the Lunenburg Historical Society to research and plan for a memorial for the
   8|                   desecrated and lost Arnold/Litch Cemetery. Gravestones were restored. A bronze plaque with the names
   9|                   of the deceased was purchased as well as granite bases and bonnets for the two (2) existing marble stones,
  10|             • joined with the Cemetery Commission to plan for the restoration of the historic Goodrich family
  11|                   cemetery,
  12|             • joined with the Historical Society to plan for the restoration and resetting of removed stone directional
  13|                   markers,
  14|             • advocated for the Community Preservation Act,
  15|             • joined with the Architectural Preservation District Commission to advocate for the preservation of the old
  16|                   Primary School and Brooks House,
  17|             • wrote and published articles in the Lunenburg Ledger pertaining to local history,
  18|                 initiated discussion and began action on completing the town survey of historic homes,
  19|                   designed and purchased signs to designate the Lunenburg National Historic District, which were erected
  20|                   by the DPW,
  21|                  the town's standing stone directional markers received their annual inspection,
  22|                   the commission advocated successfully for the preservation of the 1755 Saltbox Colonial at 62 Elm St
  23|                   and its wonderful post and beam barn through the demolition delay process.
  24|                   Since the turn of the century Lunenburg has lost historic houses at an average rate of one (1) per year.
  25|                   Without strong advocacy and public support, at the present rate of destruction, few historical homes and
  26|                   buildings will be left in Lunenburg in 100 years.
  27|                   Cullen Dwyer resigned from the commission. We thank him for his commitment and salient points of
  28|                   view that were so important to our deliberations and consensus. His work as recording secretary was
  29|                   essential, well done and appreciated by the members.
  30|                   Chair Rebecca Lantry also resigned. She had more than 15 years of service to Lunenburg as a member of
  31|                   the commission and then serving as Chair. Her advocacy and enthusiasm for the value of Lunenburg's
  32|                   past, its living history of houses, buildings, structures, landscapes and documents has been inspirational. I
  33|                   am grateful to have served with her all these years and have learned from her.
  34|                   The Commission has gained one new member, Kevin Boussom, a communications major at FSU. We
  35|                   welcome his youth and talent.
  36|             Presently, the Commission has four (4) members.
  37|             Submitted by
  38|             Richard McGrath, Chairman

===PAGE 67=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                        INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY DIRECTOR
   2|              The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency with technology.
   3|              The IT Department enables all other town departments and schools to communicate and collaborate,
   4|              manage daily tasks, organize, store and share information, and generally conduct Town business more
   5|              effectively. We also provide the necessary infrastructure, equipment, software and expertise to comply
   6|              with current regulations and methodology when interacting with outside agencies and organizations.
   7|              As we reflect on this last year, to say 2020 has been challenging would be an understatement. With the
   8|              arrival of the coronavirus in the spring, all our IT priorities shifted to an intense focus on responding to this
   9|              emergency and how best to support the town moving to remote work almost overnight while still
  10|              maintaining our core services. This report provides information on the Technology Team, as well as some
  11|              highlights of the work we have done in response to COVID-19.
  12|              The Technology Team
  13|             Steve Malandrinos - Information Technology Director
  14|             • Over eight years with the Town of Lunenburg
  15|              Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA and Belchertown
  16|              Public Schools
  17|              Daniel Nadareski - Network Administrator
  18|             • Retired police officer from the Albany, NY police force with over 24 years on the job
  19|              Prior experience includes computer crime and forensics, network administration, video security system
  20|              administration, and IP phone system administration
  21|              Neil Trahan - Remote Meeting Coordinator
  22|             • Also serves as Director of Technology for Grafton Public Schools
  23|              Works part time managing several Zoom remote meeting accounts, including scheduling, starting and in
  24|              some cases moderating public meetings to ensure things run smoothly
  25|              Along with serving as helpdesk for all users' daily technology issues and performing regular maintenance
  26|              and scheduled replacement of the town's and schools' technology assets, the IT Department shifted focus to
  27|              enable and support town departments, committees, boards and commissions to work and meet remotely.
  28|              Online meetings have become a big part of all our lives, both for internal discussions as well as public
  29|              meetings. We have worked closely with Public Access to facilitate live streaming, and have hired a part
  30|              time Remote Meeting Coordinator to keep our many remote public meetings organized.
  31|              To enable town employees to work remotely, we have also purchased laptops, upgraded internet
  32|              connectivity, and fortified our VPN solution, in many cases using grant funding available to local
  33|              governments. Phone systems were adjusted to forward calls to employees' homes or cellular phones during
  34|              times when they would not be in the office, and many pages on our website were updated to reflect new
  35|              hours or new ways of contacting various departments around town.
  36|              The past year has had an immense impact on all our lives, the Technology Team included. Our ability to
  37|              adapt to rapidly changing requirements was put to the test, and I am proud to say in most cases we rose to
  38|             the task. We share the hope that 2021 will eventually bring some normalcy back to our lives, but regardless
  39|              we will provide the tools necessary for our staff, boards and commissions to continue their important work
  40|              serving the residents of Lunenburg.
  41|                                                                                               67

===PAGE 68=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                         Lunenburg Public Library
   2|               Albert Einstein is credited with the statement: "The only thing you absolutely have to know is the location
   3|               of the library." And after 2020, our Lunenburg Public Library is in more virtual locations than ever, making
   4|               it easier to access resources in our community. Our fiscal year 2020 could hardly have held more stark
   5|               contrasts. Our circulation went from being some of the highest rates in years to our doors being closed
   6|               during the spring and the pandemic. The unexpected challenges which arose were met by our library staff's
   7|               creativity, adaptability, and problem-solving. And in response to those challenges, we have vastly expanded
   8|               our virtual presence for the library. So, even if you don't know the physical location of the library in
   9|               Lunenburg, you should be able to easily find us with an internet connection.
  10|               In a year that concluded with the rise of the COVID-19 pandemic in the world, our library responded
  11|               quickly and consistently to continue our services during unprecedented change and challenge. Just like the
  12|               people we serve, our staff demonstrated strength and resilience as we put our community's safety first while
  13|               continuing to meet the needs of that community.
  14|               Between July 2019 and February 2020, the circulation of our physical material was 14% higher than the
  15|               same period a year previously, on track to be the highest in years. During the same period the number of
  16|               visitors rose only slightly. That means people were borrowing more books per visit than the year before and
  17|               that the library improved our selections to provide even more books and materials that are relevant and
  18|               important to our community.
  19|               In March 2020, we closed our doors to the public in response to the pandemic, and our staff began to adapt
  20|               our services to be delivered virtually. Though we did not circulate physical material while we were closed,
  21|               our electronic resources saw a huge jump in use. At the conclusion of June 2020, our e-book and e-
  22|               audiobook usage was 150% higher than the previous year as a result of the change in behavior during the
  23|               last three months of the fiscal year.
  24|               During the time we were closed, we funneled more money in our electronic resources. We purchased
  25|               additional licenses for e-books and e-audiobooks and made them available through the Overdrive Libby
  26|               app. We also relied on the Friends of the Lunenburg Public Library to purchase several additional electronic
  27|               resources, including Beanstack and CreativeBug. Beanstack allows us to organize and administer virtual
  28|               reading challenges, while CreativeBug is a collection art and craft videos and courses. Both have been very
  29|               useful and beneficial to the library community.
  30|               One of the highlights of the year has to be the result of a collaboration that started before the pandemic and
  31|               concluded while we were closed to the public. Hannaford Corporation and their community support
  32|               program donated $3,500 to a renovation project that has resulted in a much improved children's area. We
  33|               added a new shelving unit, combined with new seating arrangements and display space that has created a
  34|               "tween" area that should provide a safe and welcoming space for those in-between "tween-agers" who are
  35|               too young for the teen room, but may not have felt fully comfortable in a "children's" room.
  36|               All in all, the challenges and obstacles of the pandemic represent an opportunity for the library to better
  37|               position itself for our community. We hope that our increased virtual presence will continue to be beneficial
  38|               to our users. Our staff has continued to work incredibly hard to adapt and to serve. We are here for you and
  39|               we thank all of our community partners and look forward to continuing to improve!
  40|                                                                                               68

===PAGE 69=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|              Adult Services
   2|              During summer reading, we hosted a virtual Summer Reading Challenge on Beanstack that saw 26 adult
   3|              readers log nearly 25,000 minutes of reading. Because of that success, we will continue to use Beanstack as
   4|               we move forward. It has been a wonderful new service to add to our repertoire.
   5|              One of our focuses before and during the pandemic has been on reference service, a traditional resource in
   6|              most libraries that has been underutilized in Lunenburg in recent times. Having a dedicated Adult Services
   7|              Librarian has been beneficial in this process, allowing us to help users answers questions when they reach out
   8|              to us. In addition, we have been adding to our capacity help readers find new books to read. We curate
   9|              reading recommendations and personalized library book bundles to patrons who return a short survey that can
  10|              be found on our website. This service has been successful while library browsing has been less accessible and
  11|              we hope that it will continue to expand in usefulness.
  12|              Overall, the slate of programs and services from the Adult Services department may look quite different from
  13|              past years, but we continue to work toward the goal of connecting people to their community and information
  14|              needs, an objective that will remain constant.
  15|                    FY21 Goals for Adult Services:
  16|              • To provide more adult programming on Zoom and other virtual platforms; in addition to resuming in-
  17|                    person events when it is safe to do so
  18|              • To continue supporting adult literacy through focused reading challenges using the program software
  19|                    Beanstack
  20|              • To grow the Adult Summer Reading program by partnering with more local community institutions and
  21|                    businesses
  22|              • To develop more innovative ways to connect our patrons with information resources (e.g., personalized
  23|                    kits, recommendation surveys, discussion or book clubs, etc.)
  24|              Children's Services
  25|              Our Children's Services Department has also been quite successful in making adjustments in response to the
  26|              pandemic. Within days of town facilities being closed, the library moved to virtual mode in providing
  27|              programming and outreach to our young patrons. Engagement with these programs was strong--kids were
  28|              excited for weekly story times and chapter book reading! Our early success has led us to continue to offer
  29|              weekly story times and craft kits, virtual STEM programming, monthly LPL Kids Book Club and a variety of
  30|              virtual cooking and craft programming. We have offered take-home kits which included books, activities,
  31|              crafts and recipes, and we gave out hundreds of kits!
  32|              Our summer reading program for youth was also virtual. We saw 58 young participants who logged more
  33|              than 36,000 minutes reading throughout the summer and earned 600 Beanstack badges! This early success
  34|              has led us to continue to offer challenges on Beanstack for young readers.
  35|              We will continue to adapt and expand our programs and collaborations!
  36|                    FY21 Goals for Children's Services:
  37|              • To provide more programming on Zoom and other virtual platforms; in addition to tried-and-true
  38|                    traditionally formatted programs
  39|              • To provide early literacy support to parents, caregivers, and teachers
  40|                                                                                               69

  10|             The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to manage the
  11|             Public Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town's only cable-
  12|             service provider.
  13|             The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-
  14|             broadcast programming from both town, school functions and meetings, as well as programming from
  15|             publicly available sources.
  16|             Programming
  17|             CH9 - Public
  18|             Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 64% of the local programming on Ch9 in FY20, up 1% from 63% in
  19|             FY19. The total number of Public (Ch9) programs, both local and AccessShare (shared resources), increased
  20|             from 128 in FY19 to 140 in FY20.
  21|             Local programs included high school sports, band concerts and school events up until March of 2020. The
  22|             remaining 36% of programming was obtained through other media sources. PAC relied heavily on outside
  23|             programming during the last quarter of FY20 due to COVID19.

  17|             this outlet, including Democracy NOW, Governor Baker's COVID 19 Updates and Legal Ease.
  18|             In FY20, PAC began sharing their own locally produced programs with the MassAccess content database.
  19|             The results were outstanding! Here in Massachusetts, Lunenburg's "Talkin' Baseball" episodes were and
  20|              continue to be downloaded by over 20 media centers including Avon, Bourne, Boxford, Braintree, Danvers,
  21|              Dracut, East Bridgewater, Fall River, Hadley, Lakeville, Lower Cape, Middleboro, Provincetown, Ware,
  22|              Wakefield, Westwood and Weymouth. In addition to the Massachusetts centers, these episodes were also
  23|             downloaded for airing by out of state media centers including Newport, RI and Saint Albans, VT and Oradell
  24|             and Westfield, NJ. Because of the popularity of "Talkin' Baseball" many more locally produced shows have
  25|              been added to the database.

   1|               From March to June of FY20, committee and board meetings were prohibited from meeting at Town Hall.
   2|              Therefore, all meetings were conducted via Zoom. PAC purchased a streaming service software (CASTUS
   3|               Stream) to ensure live meeting broadcasts were sent to the server from the Zoom feed for airing.
   4|               Videographers were provided with detailed instructions and training.
   5|               Each committee and board, whether broadcast live or not, records their Zoom meeting to the cloud.
   6|              This resulted in PAC offering more meetings for re-broadcast on Ch8 and YouTube, including Parks
   7|              Commission, Capital Planning, Board of Assessors, Council on Aging and TCP Building Design to name a
   8|              tew.
   9|                      All recorded Government meetings are accessible on the Public Access YouTube channel.
  10|                                                http://www.youtube.com/LunenburgAccess
  11|                                  A live feed from Ch8 on PAC's YouTube Channel is accessible by going to
  12|                                                http://www.youtube.com/c/LunenburgAccess/live
  13|              CH99 - Educational
  14|              In May of FY20, PAC added an Educational Channel, Comcast Channel 99. The additional channel has
  15|              allowed PAC to feature local educational content to viewers including bi-monthly School Committee
  16|              meetings. With the decline of town and school activity at the end of FY20, the channel's content has been
  17|              mainly populated by downloaded programming, such as Open Mind Storytime, Fur Fins and Feathers, Chess
  18|              Chat, and Science Now.
  19|              More original educational programming will be added in FY21.
  20|              Enterprise Fund
  21|              In FY20 PAC changed its financial structure from a Revenue based account to an Enterprise account. This
  22|              resulted in a new method in the process and approval of the budget. Firstly, it allows for the monthly
  23|              infusion of interest on the money held in the PAC account, added to the current quarterly Comcast revenue.
  24|               And secondly, the budget can be set with the availability of cash expected to be deposited. That is, monies
  25|              can be earmarked for expenses before they are received.
  26|              Studio and Equipment Upgrades
  27|              PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013. We encourage the public to take advantage
  28|              of the facilities and equipment offered
  29|              The strategy for the PAC studio in the upcoming fiscal years includes acquiring new studio space as soon as
  30|              municipal space becomes available. A new committee, TC Passios Building Design Committee, was formed
  31|              in FY19 to explore the reuse of the TC Passios school for Town Offices and a PAC studio.
  32|              PAC has been working with the committee's architect to help design a future studio. This studio would
  33|              provide a more permanent and comfortable environment for producing programming. A completion date has
  34|              not been set on the project, and must gain town approval at the Annual Town Meeting in FY21 to proceed to
  35|              the next steps.

   1|             Upgrades in FY20 included the replacement of the studio TriCaster switcher, from a TC455 to a TriCaster

   3|             committee/board meetings when in-person meetings resume in the future.

  11|             Staff, Producers and Volunteers
  12|             In FY20, there was one full-time Manager and 5 part-time contract videographers, classified as Vendors
  13|             (Budget Line Item: "Purchase of Services"). However, in the 4" quarter, videographers were reclassified to
  14|             non-benefitted part-time town employees. This change in classification resulted in the addition of a new
  15|             budget line-item, Payroll Videographers, and streamlined the scheduling, payroll and production process.
  16|             The PAC Manager continues to administer the day-to-day operation of the organization, including all
  17|             production and programming schedules, staff scheduling, equipment and services procurement, studio and
  18|             inventory administration, advertising, social media and 24/7 troubleshooting of on-air programming.
  19|             Videographers provided video recordings of committee meetings and additional video assignments as needed.
  20|             A technical contract consultant works with the PAC manager to provided technical and training assistance as
  21|             needed. It is expected that he will remain working for PAC through FY21. However, PAC will be searching,

  28|             PAC is always looking for producers. If a resident has a special skill or interest, PAC will help make the

  30|             Much effort has gone into recruiting volunteers, with little response. Social media and newspapers have been
  31|             used to promote volunteerism with little effect. PAC will continue to look for other ways to reach out to the

===PAGE 76=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|             Summary
   2|             FY20 ended as a difficult year for PAC, and the community at large, due to COVID19. What started out as a
   3|             successful year, soon suffered because of the lack of local activities and events. PAC was on target at the
   4|             beginning of FY20 to surpass the FY19 local programming (Ch9) growth. While the number of programs
   5|             increased, the percentage of local programs declined by 1%. However, it became necessary to change gears at
   6|             the end of the year, to ensure continuity of Government meetings (Ch8) amidst the "new normal."
   7|             The end of FY20 brought new beginnings, as an Educational Channel (99) was added. This addition has made
   8|             PAC a true Public, Education and Government (PEG) media center. The channel focuses on learning and its
   9|             content is targeted toward local education. Because the school year ended with remote learning, there wasn't a
  10|             lot of educational material to add to the programming. However, in the meantime, the schedule has been
  11|             populated with outstanding programming downloaded from the MassAccess Telvue database. As school
  12|             activities hopefully increase in FY21, you will see more Lunenburg educational activity.
  13|             Changing from a revenue account system to an Enterprise Fund in FY20 was a major change for the better.
  14|             This adjustment allowed for PAC to plan for expenses with revenue that was expected to come in, rather than
  15|             only use funds immediately available. Not only did it change the budget process as a whole, but made
  16|             planning for unexpected expenses much easier. Funds received from Comcast and account interest payments
  17|             are PAC's only source of revenue.
  18|             Improvements make way for future success. Upgrade of equipment and purchase of new equipment has
  19|             brought PAC to another level. The new equipment added to the Town Hall control room for Zoom meetings
  20|             has changed the video landscape. Now committee member will have the choice of attending meetings in-
  21|             person at Town Hall or remotely. Studio improvements are slow but steady, as we have upgraded the
  22|             production switcher and plan additional improvements in FY21. We don't know what the future of video
  23|             delivery will be, but PAC is prepared whatever the scenario.
  24|             Moving forward PAC hopes to continue to bring residents a consistent line-up of programming on each of our
  25|             three PEG channels. The growth of content on Channel 8 (Gov't) due to Zoom recordings has been a
  26|             consequence of the pandemic. Municipal meetings not broadcast live are uploaded to YouTube and Channel 8
  27|             from Zoom files, offering more informational content to viewers. The Public Channel (9) has continued to
  28|             grow as shared resources, and limited local productions, keep a steady flow of updated material. And, the new
  29|             Educational Channel (99), in its infancy, is delivering programs of interest for all.
  30|             Lunenburg Public Access (PAC) is your source of all things local. Won't you join us as a producer or
  31|             volunteer?
  32|             PAC's supportive staff offers production assistance, making it easy to produce your own show. Our
  33|             community is thriving with talented and creative individuals - now is your time to shine! If producing doesn't
  34|             appeal to you, then consider "behind the camera" options. Become a volunteer. Volunteers are the "life blood"
  35|             of media centers around the state, and without that involvement PAC will not be able to grow. Consider
  36|             volunteering to help provide the necessary resources needed for PAC's continued success.
  37|             We need you!
  38|                                                                                               76

   1|                                                     SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
   2|                                                               JANUARY 1, 2020 - NOVEMBER 1, 2020
   3|              The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Committee is a five member committee appointed by the Select Board.
   4|              2020 Committee Members
   5|              Susan Doherty, Chairperson; Julie Belliveau, Vice-Chairperson; Sheila Craigen, Clerk; Laura Boussom,
   6|              Member at Large
   7|              What is the Senior Property Tax Work-Off?
   8|              The Senior Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an opportunity to
   9|              have an abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants work in various town departments doing
  10|             jobs such as general office work, custodial, library shelving, Meals on Wheels, kitchen assistance duties, etc.,
  11|              to earn the tax abatement.
  12|              • The 2020 tax abatement maximum is $1,500 and applied to your 3'd quarter real estate tax bill.
  13|                    Eligibility Requirements
  14|              • Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  15|              • Must occupy and be the owner of the property as of July 1st
  16|              • Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse's name (60 years+)
  17|              • Meet the income guidelines of (proof of income is required):
  18|                        one person household - $50,000
  19|                        two person household - $75,000
  20|              • Earnings are exempt from state taxes
  21|              • Earnings are subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional
  22|                    Retirement or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement
  23|              • Final amount earned will be applied to the 3rd                          quarter real estate tax
  24|              • All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check
  25|              • All Property Tax Workers must re-apply and be accepted annually for participation in the program
  26|              2020 Participation
  27|              During 2020, there were seven residents who participated in the Program in the following departments:
  28|              • Board of Health
  29|              • Building Department/Zoning
  30|              • Council on Aging
  31|              • DPW/Sewer Departments
  32|              • Public Library
  33|              • Tax Collector
  34|              • Assessors
  35|              These individuals as a whole, accumulated 523.75 hours amounting to $6,678.84 in real estate tax
  36|              abatements.
  37|                           For more information or to obtain an application, please contact Julie Belliveau, Assistant Town
  38|                         Manager/HR Director at 978-582-4130 x150 or jbelliveau@lunenburgonline.com
  39|                                                                                               77

===PAGE 78=== (ocr, 52 lines)
   1|                                                                            SEWER COMMISSION
   2|               The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the
   3|               future, and developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies. Under the Town's Charter,
   4|               responsibility for the day-to-day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the
   5|               supervision of the Town Manager who delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain the operational
   6|               side of the system to the Department of Public Works' Director. The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a
   7|               sewer treatment plant. Approximately 85% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster and
   8|               the other 15% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
   9|               The five-member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6,
  10|               2006. In August 2019, John Reynolds resigned from the Commission and there were 2 vacancies. Former
  11|               Commissioners Dave MacDonald and Mike Nault stepped up to fill the positions on September 3, 2019. At
  12|               the September 24, 2019 meeting, the Commission elected Joan Pease as Chair, Ryan Stober as Vice-Chair
  13|               and Carl Luck as Clerk. In January 2020, Joan and Ryan gave their resignations due to personal reasons,
  14|               and Carl Luck became acting Chair. The Commission welcomed back John Reynolds and new member
  15|               Brett Ramsden in February 2020. With a full board, the Commission elected new officers on 3/10/20 -
  16|               Dave MacDonald-Chair, John Reynolds-Vice Chair and Brett Ramsden-Clerk.
  17|               The Covid-19 crisis resulted in shutdowns during March 2020. The Town of Lunenburg closed all town
  18|               offices with employees working from home. In June offices reopened by appointment only. All meetings
  19|               were held virtually via Zoom for the rest of 2020. The Town Caucus was cancelled and the Town Meeting
  20|               and Town Election were rescheduled to June. The Commission had 4 openings on the Town Election ballot
  21|               and the following were elected: Dave MacDonald - 1 yr. term, John Reynolds- 2 yr. term and Brett
  22|               Ramsden and Mike Deveikis - 3 yr. terms. At the June 23, 2020 meeting, the Commission re-elected Dave
  23|               MacDonald-Chair, John Reynolds-Vice Chair and Brett Ramsden-Clerk.
  24|               In July 2019, the Commission began the negotiations with Small Water Systems Services (SWSS) for the
  25|               FY20 continuation of the contract for the Operations and Management (O&M) of the 10 pumping stations,
  26|               emergency generators and sewer infrastructure. There were concerns about the increase in callouts and that
  27|               the pump stations were not running as reliably as in the past. The extra repairs generated invoices that
  28|               exceeded the contracted amount. The Commission met with SWSS in November 2019 and February 2020
  29|               to iron out issues with the service and invoicing. In March 2020, SWSS notified the Commission of their
  30|               intent to terminate their contract effective April 9, 2020. DP W Director Jack Rodriquenz secured Chad T.
  31|               Empey LLC(CTE) as interim O&M contractor. CTE employee, Al Packard, took over maintenance of the
  32|               pump stations and reliability greatly improved, with few callouts. In April 2020, the Commission began
  33|               working on a scope of services for the RFP for the FY21 O&M Contract and put it out for bid in July 2020.
  34|                There are currently 898 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 340 which have access
  35|               to Town Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY20, 24 new accounts connected to Town Sewer. The
  36|               Commission billed out $864,269 in quarterly sewer bills and had $44,864 of prior year delinquencies added
  37|               to the owners' property tax bills for non-payment. Four properties were assessed a privilege fee in FY20.
  38|               Three properties assessed were residential. One assessment was for a commercial building. Privilege fees
  39|               help to pay down the debt owed by the Town on the original sewer project.
  40|               During FY20, the Commission worked with the residents at 189 Cross Road for a sewer extension, which
  41|               was approved at the Fall Town Meeting. The Commission is always willing to discuss a potential sewer
  42|               extension when the applicant can show that challenges exist for standard septic systems.
  43|               The new Irrigation Policy went into effect on August 1, 2019, requiring participants to upgrade to a radio
  44|               capable meter by July 1, 2020. All Irrigation Meter Program customers will be charged an annual fee of
  45|               $50 to participate but can receive credit on their quarterly sewer bills based on their Irrigation meter
  46|               readings. In April 2020, in light of the Covid-19 crisis, the Commission voted to move the deadline for
  47|               installing radio-compatible equipment to September 1, 2020.
  48|               In FY19, the Sewer Commission contracted with Wright Pierce to develop a Maintenance Plan to insure the
  49|               smooth operation of all 10 sewer pump stations. Wright Pierce conducted a thorough evaluation of the
  50|               equipment and maintenance currently done at the pump stations. A draft Maintenance Plan, which included
  51|               an asset management spreadsheet for tracking all maintenance performed, was presented to the Commission
  52|                                                                                               78

===PAGE 79=== (ocr, 51 lines)
   1|              in December 2019. With input from the Commission, Town Manager and the DPW director, Wright
   2|              Pierce finalized the plan in spring 2020.
   3|              The Sewer Commission developed a scope of work for an Inflow/Infiltration (I/I) 3rd party review, aimed
   4|              at identifying the best use of resources to lessen the V/I entering the sewer system. The project was
   5|              awarded to Woodard & Curran and work started January 2020. The Woodard & Curran team compiled
   6|              data from the pump stations and past projects to determine possible sources of VI. They presented their
   7|              findings on 6/9/20 to the Commission. A final report with recommendations and an Excel billing tool will
   8|              be presented in FY21.
   9|              In FY20, the Commission continued the annual permit program for the 20 Food Service Establishments
  10|               with grease traps and interceptors on Town Sewer. Due to the financial strain on restaurant owners due to
  11|              the Covid-19 crisis, the Commission voted to waive the Annual Permit fee for the Grease Trap Program.
  12|              The Commission continues to require regular cleanings and submittal of maintenance logs from these
  13|              establishments as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and
  14|              maintenance costs of the sewer system. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial
  15|              disposal, individual homeowners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which
  16|              increases everyone's costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
  17|              At the June 2020 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward 4 articles, 3 of which related to the FY21
  18|              budget and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY21, $790,027.63 was appropriated
  19|              from Sewer Enterprise revenues and the sum of $125,650.25 was appropriated from Retained Earnings
  20|              along with transfers from 2 stabilization funds (Capital Reserve $20,000 and Reserve Capacity $7,513.66)
  21|              for a total appropriation of $943,191.54 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY21. The amount of
  22|              $877,011.90 was approved to cover both the Enterprise Fund's share of Town services and debt service on
  23|              the sewer betterments. The second article transferred monies to the Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund in
  24|              the amount of $30,000, approximately 3% of proposed revenues as the Commission had done for FY18
  25|              and FY19. This fund is to be used to maintain and improve the operating etticiency of the system or cover
  26|              large emergency expenditures. The third warrant article was to transfer the sum of $656.00 into the OPEB
  27|              Sewer Trust Fund. All three articles passed at Town Meeting and the fourth article was withdrawn. It was
  28|              determined in FY20 that any contributions into the Stabilization Funds based on income received the prior
  29|              fiscal year would be acted on at the Special Town Meeting following the close of the fiscal year. The two
  30|              funds affected by this change are the Sewer Bank-Inflow/Infiltration Stabilization Fund and the Reserve
  31|              Capacity Stabilization Fund. A portion of the connection charges assessed at the time of an application for
  32|              a permit to connect go into these accounts and target resolving I/I issues and future capacity costs paid to
  33|              the cities of Fitchburg and Leominster.
  34|              The Sewer Commission has worked diligently to maintain a balanced budget every year and had not raised
  35|              rates since 2013. However due to increased costs for operating the sewer pump stations and increased
  36|              costs for discharging to Fitchburg and Leominster systems, the Commission passed a 10% rate increase
  37|              for FY21.
  38|              The Commission continued working in FY20 with Adam Costa, (Town Counsel), of Mead, Talerman and
  39|               Costa, and thanks him for his guidance in contract negotiations and clarification of regulations. The
  40|               Commission would also like to thank Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director, for the seamless and smooth
  41|              operation of the Sewer Division of the DPW.
  42|              They would also like to acknowledge and thank the Lunenburg Water District for their partnership and
  43|              good working relationship with the Sewer Division in providing valuable assistance with meter readings,
  44|              ownership changes, and status updates.
  45|              Sewer Commission as of FY 20 report submittal (Feb 2021):
  46|               Dave MacDonald, Chair
  47|              John Reynolds, Vice Chair
  48|              Brett Ramsden, Clerk
  49|               Carl Luck, Member
  50|              Mike Deveikis, Member

   1|                                                                               BOARD OF HEALTH
   2|              The Board of Health Office is located in the old Ritter Library building on the corner of Mass. Ave. and
   3|              Leominster Road. Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right.
   4|              The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued its
   5|              mission to maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2020, reviewing Title V Inspection
   6|              Reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing inspections, complaints
   7|              and general public health related issues as well as resident concerns.
   8|              In addition, shortly after the beginning of the year the Board was tasked with responding to the emerging COVID
   9|             -19 pandemic. In accordance with Governor Baker's Emergency Orders, we are tasked with implementing and
  10|              enforcing emergency orders. The Board of Health assisted residents and businesses in understanding ever
  11|              changing guidance and compliance with those orders. We conducted compliance checks and responded to
  12|              complaints. As the pandemic continued our office assisted residents and business in understanding testing,
  13|              quarantine and necessary compliance. Because of its size, Nashoba Associated Boards of Health had been able
  14|             to get limited amounts of vaccine and has been able to hold several clinics, vaccinating First Responders, i.e.
  15|              Police, Firemen and EMT personnel from each town while following State requirements. Clinics have also
  16|              been held for residents over 75 years of age or who suffer 2 or more debilitating medical conditions. In the
  17|              meantime, follow recommendations. Wash hands frequently, wear a mask, and keep six feet rule.
  18|              Homeland Security and Emergency Preparedness planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between our
  19|              agents, other Town departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our
  20|              partner towns in our region.
  21|              This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new wells,
  22|             repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits. The Board continues its vigilance with regard to septic
  23|              systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its general mandate to protect
  24|              the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
  25|              The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the Nashoba
  26|              Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, nursing services are provided to our
  27|             residents. This year clinics provided our residents with inoculations for Flu.
  28|              The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project. Mosquito Control
  29|              monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as Eastern Equine
  30|              Encephalitis Virus and West Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer
  31|              times of the year. Residents who want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to:
  32|              cттср@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, completing a request on their webpage at: http://
  33|              www.cmmcp.org, or calling the Board of Health office. This service is free of charge to residents.
  34|              With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to residents recovering from
  35|              illness and Hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service. Please contact our office in the Ritter
  36|              Building for more information.
  37|              Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the Citizens of
  38|              Lunenburg. If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the
  39|              Board office at 582-4146 ext. 430.

   1|                                                                           FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2|                                                                            LUNENBURG      FIRE
   3|                                                                                   SETTLED 1700 ІСОДРОКАТКО
   4|                                                                                         DEPT
   5|                                                                               FIRE RESCUE
   6|                The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated
   7|               to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of
   8|                                                                             the Town of Lunenburg.
   9|                          In 2020, your Fire Department responded to 1955 calls for service, broken down as follows:
  10|                          Fires, Alarms & Hazardous Conditions 489                                                  Emergency Medical                  962
  11|                          Inspections 534                                                                           Other                               18
  12|                          The 2020 Pandemic created one of the most challenging and stressful years in anyone's memory.
  13|               Our personnel did not have the option of "Staying at Home." We had to make drastic changes in how we
  14|               treated patients and interacted with the public, while still providing the level of service our community has
  15|               come to expect. This was done while dealing with rapidly changing, and often conflicting information from
  16|               State and Federal officials. That we experienced only four members testing positive is a testament to
  17|               everyone's dedication and commitment to serve our community. My thanks go out to the men and women
  18|               of the Department who persevered through this strange time, and continue to do so today.
  19|                          Current Fire Department staffing is now 7 Career and 40-45 On Call Firefighters and Emergency
  20|               Medical Technicians, plus 1 full-time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator. The station is manned 7
  21|               days per week with 2-3 Firefighter/Emergency Medical Technicians. This coverage is a combination of
  22|               Career personnel in conjunction with 88 hours per week of per diem coverage. This coverage is provided
  23|               by our Call Firefighter/EMTs staffing the station at their hourly rate. Staffing the station with dual trained
  24|               Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency occurs.
  25|                          Though we have 24-hour coverage in the station, many EMS calls and nearly all fire calls still
  26|               require more personnel than are on duty in the station. This means we still rely heavily on the dedication
  27|               and commitment of our On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians. Our Call personnel
  28|               remain, and will remain a vital part of our emergency response for years to come. A new Call Firefighter/
  29|               EMT must attend over 340 hours of initial training, plus additional time in orientation and field training.
  30|               Members qualifying to drive Fire Apparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of classroom and
  31|               practical training. On average, it takes a new member one and a half to two years to full complete this
  32|               training.
  33|                          The department continues to move towards the goal of providing our own Paramedic Level
  34|               Ambulance Service. Though we currently operate two ambulances at the Advanced EMT Level, we must
  35|               still rely on outside agencies to provide Paramedic Level care when needed. Paramedic training is in excess
  36|               of 2000 hours and requires 1-½ years of college level classroom education, clinical time and field
  37|               internships to obtain certification. The department continues to train our newly hired staff to this level and
  38|               recruit licensed Paramedics to fill Career positions when possible.                                             We had hoped to be providing
  39|               Paramedic Level care by the beginning of 2021, but the COVID Pandemic has pushed that out to Mid-2021.
  40|                                                                                               86

===PAGE 87=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                        Members who were in the midst of their Paramedic training lost 6-7 months due to the training schools
   2|             shutting down.
   3|                        In 2020, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 534 Inspections. This is in addition to multiple fire
   4|             drills and public education initiatives. By law, we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon Monoxide
   5|              Detector installations when a home is sold. We are also required to inspect all new oil burner and propane gas
   6|             installations. Any business holding an on-premise liquor license also requires a yearly inspection for fire
   7|             safety issues. In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire
   8|             protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process. Though the Pandemic altered how
   9|             we conducted these inspections, we never halted them. Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2020
  10|             include:
  11|             • Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety
  12|                   Education in both the Schools and with Senior Citizens. This grant provides funding for both materials
  13|                   and personnel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations. Highlights of activity
  14|                   through this grant include:
  15|             • Though the Pandemic severely curtailed our interactions with the Schools and the Elderly, we m
  16|                   continued to provide services where and when we could. We look forward to returning to in person
  17|                   presentations and training in 2021.
  18|                   Personnel assisted with several community events, such as the Council on Aging and Lion's Club
  19|                   Thanksgiving Basket programs, The Salvation Army's "Rescue Christmas" initiative, and the Christmas
  20|                   Light Drive Through at the Middle/High School
  21|               Training to certify personnel as Public Life Safety Educators. In 2020, Firefighter Melissa Buchanan
  22|                  joined Captain James Ricci, Lt. Christos Lekaditis and Firefighter Austin Flagg as the department's Life
  23|                   Safety Educators.
  24|                   Participation in the Senior Center's "Sand for Seniors" Program. This program provides buckets of
  25|                   walkway sand for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months. In
  26|                   partnership with this program, Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when possible,
  27|                   inspects their homes for Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detectors. If the resident was lacking detectors,
  28|                   Capt. Ricci is able to install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant. Despite the Pandemic,
  29|                   we modified our procedures to keep this valuable program operating.
  30|             It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke
  31|             detectors.
  32|             Seven Firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical Rescue and Dive
  33|             Teams. These regional teams respond to high risk and unusual rescue situations, such as Confined Space,
  34|             Rescue from Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and Underwater Search and Recovery.
  35|             In conjunction with            • these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue Unit and a Collapse/Trench
  36|              Rescue Trailer.
  37|              Apparatus replacement continues to move forward.                                     The replacements for Ladder 1 and Engine 4 were
  38|             received in July and October respectively. These units are now in full service and will serve the Town for
  39|             many years to come. This puts the average age of our front line firefighting apparatus at a little over 6 years
  40|             old. Newer, state of the art apparatus will decrease our maintenance costs and down time. It also provides
  41|             safer and more reliable equipment for emergency response.
  42|                         In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their
  43|             outstanding commitment to the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to impress
  44|             me every day. Especially in the face of one of the most stressful years in anyone's memory. I would also like
  45|             to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions for their continuing help. The level of
  46|             interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in the daily course of town business in invisible to many people, but
  47|             it is critical to all operations. Finally, my thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing
  48|             support of public safety. Such support allows us to continue providing the high level of service you have
  49|              come to expect from us.
  50|                                                                                               87

===PAGE 91=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|              I am truly honored to be selected as the new Chief of Police for the Lunenburg Police Department, and it is
   2|              my pleasure to present this town report. The Lunenburg Police Department has seen many changes in 2020,
   3|              the first and foremost is the retirement of Chief James P. Marino.
   4|              Chief Marino started with the Lunenburg Police Department in 1979 as a Reserve Police Officer, rising
   5|              through the ranks in a 41 year career that culminated with being appointed as the Chief of Police on July 4,
   6|              2012. Throughout his career Chief Marino was dedicated to not only the Lunenburg Police Department, but
   7|              to the Town of Lunenburg. This dedication was evident as he tirelessly built cruisers for the Department
   8|              year after year. Chief Marino has also volunteered for as many civic events as he could. Chief Marino
   9|              continues to dedicate his time to the Town as member of the Select Board.
  10|              During his tenure, Chief Marino was assigned as one of the first Detectives for the Lunenburg Police
  11|              Department. While a Detective, Chief Marino received several commendations for his investigative work
  12|              that led to successful apprehension and prosecutions of suspects). Chief Marino was also recognized for his
  13|              work with the area drug task force when the task force was in its infancy. His role as a long time DARE
  14|              officer for the Lunenburg Public Schools made Chief Marino a recognizable figure throughout the town, and
  15|              built a lasting relationship between the School District and Lunenburg Police Department.
  16|              As recognized as he was for his diligent work as a police officer, Chief Marino's true passion was evident as
  17|              the Department's fleet manager. Chief Marino has always been a car enthusiast and carried that with him
  18|              throughout his career. Chief Marino has built the department's cruisers for over 25 years, with his attention
  19|             to detail reflected on how well built the cruisers were.
  20|              I am truly grateful that I had the opportunity to work for Chief Marino for the last 20 years. His mentorship
  21|              and friendship has led me to the position that I am in today. On behalf of the Lunenburg Police Department,
  22|              we want to thank Chief James P. Marino for all that he has done for the department and the Town of
  23|              Lunenburg. We wish him good luck, good health and prosperity as he enters the next phase of his life.
  24|              In December of 2020, Lt. Jeffrey M. Thibodeau was appointed to the permanent Lieutenant's position. Lt.
  25|              Thibodeau has been a member of the Lunenburg Police Department since 2011, and obtained the rank of
  26|              Detective Sergeant in July of 2014. Lt. Thibodeau has been a police officer since 2001 when he was hired
  27|             by the Townsend Police Department, and has also worked with the Federal Bureau of Prisons located in
  28|              Devens, MA.
  29|              Lt. Thibodeau holds a Bachelor's Degree of Science in Criminal Justice from Fitchburg State University.
  30|              During his tenure with the Lunenburg Police Department, Lt. Thibodeau has completed numerous
  31|             investigative classes and trainings, which eventually led him to be assigned to the Federal Bureau of
  32|              Investigations High Intensity Drug Trafficking Area (HIDTA), and the North Worcester County Drug Task
  33|              Force. Lt. Thibodeau has also received leadership training through HIDTA, and has completed two sessions
  34|              of the FBI LEEDA course. Lt. Thibodeau has completed several administrative courses involving Public
  35|              Records Law, Internal Affairs Investigation and other related courses since his appointment. It is a pleasure
  36|             to welcome Lt. Thibodeau as the second in command of the department.
  37|              In December of 2020, Sgt. Joshua M. Mathieu was appointed as a permanent Sergeant. Sgt. Mathieu has
  38|              been a member of the Lunenburg Police Department since August of 2011, and has been a full time officer
  39|              since November of 2012. Sgt. Mathieu holds a Bachelor Degree of Science degree from Westfield State
  40|                                                                                               91

===PAGE 92=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|               College. Sgt. Mathieu has completed numerous training courses involving investigative techniques, is one of
   2|               the department's Field Training Officers, and has been involved with training numerous new Lunenburg
   3|               Police Officers. Sgt. Mathieu is also the department's liaison for the Critical Incident Management System
   4|              (CIMS), which is a program involving a collaboration with Central MA communities to combat Opioid
   5|               addiction. It is a pleasure to welcome Sgt. Mathieu to the department's command staff.
   6|               In 2020 we had three officers complete the Massachusetts Municipal Training Committee full time Basic
   7|               Recruit Officer Training course. Off. Ramon J. Cabrera, Off. Anthony P. Cappucci, and Off. Kamerin P.
   8|               Munroe. All three officers have successfully completed the required Department Field Training, and are
   9|               currently assigned to the Patrol Bureau. We are pleased to welcome Ramon, Anthony and Kam to the
  10|               department's full time ranks.
  11|               2020 has been truly an unparalleled year, dealing with the Covid-19 Pandemic and the effects it has had on
  12|               our way of life, and our economy, to dealing with civil unrest issues that have arisen as result of the tragic
  13|               events that unfolded with the death of George Floyd. Even as these events have changed our worlds, the
  14|               Lunenburg Police Department strives to give the community the best possible service that we can provide.
  15|               During these unprecedented times our officers have performed admirably, risking their own health to
  16|               continue to serve the community. I commend our officers, and members of our department for the work that
  17|               has been completed throughout the year without reservation.
  18|               Mission Statement:
  19|               We, the members of the Lunenburg Police Department, strive to provide the highest quality of police services
  20|              to our community members and visitors alike. We believe in Community Policing, and all of the benefits that
  21|               are realized from this concept. We will strive to maintain the strong partnerships that have been developed
  22|               with our community, and continuously look for ways improve that relationship. We will strive to deliver
  23|               police services in a fair and impartial manner, consistent with all applicable laws, while respecting all
  24|              individual rights. We will use innovative and modern ideas and techniques to solve crimes, apprehend
  25|               criminal offenders, and help resolve quality of life issues.
  26|               Values:
  27|              • We are committed to ensuring that all of our actions reflect the utmost integrity, and honesty.
  28|              • We are committed to respecting individual rights, human dignity, and the values of all members of the
  29|                    community and members of the Lunenburg Police Department.
  30|               • We are a proactive and problem solving agency.
  31|               • We strive to serve our citizens in a professional manner with compassion, respect, and empathy.
  32|               • We are committed to providing the highest quality of police services to our community.
  33|              • We recognize the value of our employees, and recognize they are our most valued resource. We will
  34|                    strive to provide our employees with the necessary resources, training, and career development to
  35|                    enhance their professionalism.
  36|              • We recognize that the well being of our employees is the upmost importance. We will strive to provide
  37|                    the necessary assistance to assure our employees enjoy a long and satisfying career.

  17|               As result of the above calls the officers generated 1,095 written reports involving Incident Based Reporting
  18|              (IBR) and National Incident Based Reporting (NIBRS), which is a State and Federal Crime data base. The
  19|              officers also generated an additional 974 reports that did not fall in the either IBR or NIBRS category.
  20|              In 2020 there was an 11% increase with Crimes Against Persons, with Aggravated and Simple Assaults
  21|              accounting for highest increase at 29% and 35% respectively. Without a significant study being conducted, it
  22|              is believed that the number of assaults that have occurred is related to the Pandemic and the effects of
  23|              isolation.
  24|              In 2020 there was a 23% total increase in Crimes Against Property. Unemployment and Identity Fraud
  25|              accounted for the significant increase with Property Crimes, with the two categories having a 117% increase
  26|              from 2019.
  27|               Although property crimes have risen, there was a 15% decrease in Breaking and Entering's, and a 58%
  28|              decrease in shoplifting. These numbers also reflect the effects of the Pandemic and the fact that people are
  29|              staying home.
  30|              I would like to thank the citizens of Lunenburg for their continued support. The department could not be
  31|              successful without the support and collaboration with the citizens we serve. I look forward to leading this
  32|              department as we continue to navigate through these trying times.

   1|                                                                               PLANNING BOARD
   2|              As everyone is aware this past year was challenging. The Planning Board would like to thank the residents of
   3|             Lunenburg, business owners and applicants for their cooperation and patience as the staff and Board adjusted
   4|             to the new normal of meeting on Zoom and coordinating daily operations around the required safety
   5|             protocols and varying access to public buildings.
   6|             Fifteen (15) ANR plans (land division plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control Law, but
   7|             still requiring Board endorsement) were approved.
   8|             Butterfly Lane, a nine-lot residential subdivision on Elmwood Road, which initially began construction in
   9|             2014, was completed and the street accepted as a public way.
  10|             Tri Town Landing on Youngs Road began construction on Baker Brook Apartments (Building #5), which
  11|             would consist of 70 units of rental senior housing.
  12|             Construction was completed and occupancy granted for Lunenburg's Water District new office and garage on
  13|             Massachusetts Avenue.
  14|             The Planning Board adopted a Charge for an Economic Development Committee (EDC). This group would
  15|             be composed of a combination of Town Officials, residents and business owners charged with providing
  16|             advice, and guidance on the state of the business community in Lunenburg and ways that Town Government
  17|              can support and sustain the local economy.
  18|             Two warrant articles, amending the Earth Removal General Bylaw and transferring the permitting authority
  19|             from the Select Board to the Planning Board, were presented at June 2020 Annual Town Meeting, but did not
  20|              pass.

  22|             fill one of the vacancies until the May 2020 Town election; regrettably she had to step down after only a short
  23|             time due to job relocation. The Board and Planning office staff would like to thank Ms. Thompson, Mr.
  24|              Bittner and Ms. Lisio for their volunteerism and all the contributions they provided to making Lunenburg a
  25|             wonderful community. As a result of May 2020 Town Election, two new members joined the Board;
  26|             Matthew Brenner and Erik Torgersen. The Board and Planning Office staff welcomed Mr. Brenner and Mr.
