===PAGE 98=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|                            AD HOC OPEN SPACE ADVISORY COMMITTEE TO THE PLANNING BOARD
   2|               The Open Space Committee's efforts in FY'21 revolved around finalizing two major projects it had begun
   3|               in FY '20. Namely, finalizing the update the town's Open Space and Recreation Plan (OSRP) and
   4|               completing the acquisition of the former Saliba property on Northfield Road as unanimously approved at
   5|               the Special Town Meeting in November of 2019.
   6|               The OSRP received final approval from the Massachusetts Executive Office of Energy and Environmental
   7|               Affairs Division of Conservation Services' (DCS) on May 15, 2020. In addition to being a valuable
   8|               planning tool, the OSRP also makes the town eligible for DCS-administered grant programs through 2026.
   9|               After Special Town Meeting approval, the Open Space Committee assisted with the completion of the
  10|               Saliba acquisition, which added approximately 318 acres of land for town conservation (259 acres north of
  11|               Northfield Road, with frontage on New West Townsend Road and Howard Street) and recreation uses (59
  12|               acres on the south side of Northfield Road). The $775,000 open space acquisition was funded with the
  13|               assistance of $500,000 in reimbursements by state grants made possible by the newly approved ORP
  14|               The property was acquired on June 25, 2020. The conservation portion of the property is known as "Table
  15|               Rock Conservation Area" and the recreation portion of the property is known as "Northfield Park", both
  16|               accessible from a small parking area located on Northfield Road near Settlers' Crossing Golf Course.
  17|               The Open Space Committee also reviewed several Chapter 61 withdrawal notifications this past year as
  18|               part of the town's defined multi-board process. One such notification at 250 Howard Street resulted in a
  19|               negotiated donation of approximately 3.4 acres of land to the Conservation Commission. The donation
  20|               was approved at the November 2020 Special Town Meeting and conveyed to the Town in January of 2021.
  21|               The Open Space Committee also advised other town boards on the Bright Farms project, particularly
  22|               related to the donation of approximately 1.5 acres of land to provide critical recreational trail linkage from
  23|               the Rayno Conservation Area to the newly acquired Northfield Park and beyond. The donation was
  24|               approved at the November 2020 Special Town Meeting.
  25|               Lastly, the Open Space Committee has begun a screening process to review the inventory of existing town-
  26|               owned property to determine if any vacant and currently unused parcels are of potential conservation or
  27|               recreation interest. Parcels of interest identified in that screening process will then be brought forward to
  28|               other town boards and committees to discuss a plan for the future of those parcels.
  29|               Respectfully submitted,
  30|               Brandon Kibbe, Chairman
  31|                                                                                               98

===PAGE 99=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|                                                                    ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   2|              The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2020. There were several
   3|              applicants seeking special permits and special permits for dimensional variations. The Zoning Board
   4|             meetings are held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month at Town Hall at 7:00PM or ZOOM as
   5|              an alternative on an "as needed" basis.
   6|              The Zoning Board consists of the following members:
   7|              Alfred Gravelle, Chairman,
   8|             James Besarkarski, Vice-Chairman,
   9|              Members:
  10|              Hans Wentrup,
  11|              David Blatt,
  12|             Patrick Callahan
  13|              Anthony Nicastro, Associate Member.
  14|             Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
  15|              The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  16|              1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-
  17|                   laws.
  18|             2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-laws.
  19|              3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General
  20|                   Laws, as amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or
  21|                   enforcement action from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning
  22|                   Agency, abutting City or Town or other Administrative Official.
  23|             4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws,
  24|                   Chapter 40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to
  25|                   the Board under Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can,
  26|                   by law, circumvent local zoning ordinances.
  27|              The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts
  28|              Avenue, Lunenburg.
  29|              Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 PM Tuesday and
  30|              Thursday, closed Friday.
  31|             If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146 X 421
  32|              during regular business hours.
  33|              Zoning Board applications are available in the office and online at
  34|             https://www.lunenburgma.gov/boards-commissions-committees/zoning-board-appeals
  35|                                                                                               99

   1|                                                                           Lunenburg Public Schools
   2|                                                                                     Town Report
   3|                                                                                        2020-2021
   4|                                                                           Superintendent's Message
   5|             It is always a pleasure for me to present my annual report for the Lunenburg Public Schools, a district I am
   6|             honored and proud to lead. 2020 was a truly unprecedented year in public education. This report reflects the
   7|             ways in which the schools were impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic and the district's continued focus on
   8|             our Strategy for Continuous Improvement. I want to acknowledge and express my sincere gratitude for a
   9|             collaborative approach toward projecting and planning for current and future needs with the Town Manager,
  10|             Heather Lemieux. As the leader of the district I am fortunate to work with a School Committee that is truly
  11|             dedicated to serving the best interests of our students and appreciative that the Board of Selectmen and
  12|             Finance Committee work collaboratively with the School Committee and School Department not only to
  13|              strengthen communication, but to problem-solve in order to better meet the educational needs of the children
  14|             of Lunenburg.
  15|             The work of the district, outlined in the Strategy for Continuous Improvement, is aligned with our Mission
  16|             and Vision:
  17|             The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and
  18|             passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship,
  19|             and rewarding lives.
  20|             The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its values of
  21|             attending to the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and student-centered
  22|             instruction for deeper learning at all levels, and promoting the development of attributes and skills necessary
  23|            for continuous learning for all.
  24|             The Strategy is a living document which is reviewed annually and adjusted as needed. In August 2020, the
  25|             District Leadership Team reviewed that Strategy, its objectives and action plans, with a "COVID-19 needs"
  26|             lens. It was determined that no new COVID-specific objectives or priority actions were needed. The
  27|              Strategy objectives for the next three to four years are:
  28|                         Transforming Teaching and Learning: To ensure teaching and learning are student-centered
  29|                        and inclusive by: (1) establishing effective teams of educational leaders to accomplish the essential
  30|                        work for transforming practice, and (2) hiring and developing excellent educators who are continually
  31|                        learning and adapting practice to meet the needs of all students. (DSP Goals: 1, 2, 3, 5, {4, 7})
  32|                         Cultivating Climate & Culture: To create a culture that values data use and fosters continuous
  33|                        learning in a climate that is safe, caring, inclusive and reflective of our core values. (District Strategic
  34|                        Plan Goals 1, 6)
  35|                        Communication & Operations: To improve operational efficiencies, including communication,
  36|                        in order to increase the organization's capacity to execute the district's mission and vision. (District
  37|                        Strategic Plan Goal 8)
  38|                                                                                              102

===PAGE 103=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                          Equity, Access, Achievement & Opportunity for All Students: To ensure all students are
   2|                          provided equal access to a high-quality education, that is collaborative, interactive, and relevant with
   3|                          an appropriate level of support and challenge so that they can become well-rounded, resilient,
   4|                          confident, and capable young adults adequately prepared for future challenges. (District Strategic
   5|                          Plan Goals 1, 6)
   6|              In order to cultivate district-wide leadership in key areas identified in the Strategy for Continuous
   7|              Improvement, the district continues to create teams to lead our work. In 2018-2019, the Behavioral Health
   8|               Advisory Council, Elementary Literacy Team, and Professional Development Committee were created. In
   9|              2019-2020, a District Advisory Council and Equity Advisory Council were formed. In addition to existing
  10|              building-based Teacher Support Teams (TST), in 2020-2021 building-based Behavioral Health Support
  11|              Teams (BHST) were established. These teams review student cases referred by staff or administration. TST
  12|              determines which academic interventions may be needed and BHST determines which social/emotional/
  13|              behavioral interventions are needed. Intervention plans are developed, implemented and reviewed for
  14|              impact and progress. In 2020-2021, an Interim Director of Teaching and Learning and Title I Coordinator
  15|              joined the Instructional Leadership Team (ILT) which is focused on improving teaching and learning. Our
  16|              Behavioral Health Coordinator, new this year, Extended Day Director; School-Community Linked Services
  17|              Coordinator; along with our new ILT members, have joined our District Leadership Team in order to
  18|              establish effective communication within the district, particularly important under COVID conditions.
  19|              In March 2020, school buildings across the Commonwealth were closed and teaching and learning
  20|              transitioned to a virtual format. This presented several challenges for the district: feeding our students,
  21|              ensuring they had means to access online learning, and shifting instruction to a virtual platform. We
  22|              immediately set to work and created a grab-n-go drive through breakfast and lunch program, making
  23|              deliveries to homes if families did not have the means to pick-up the meals at the school. We gathered
  24|              together every laptop and iPad in the district, surveyed families to determine the level of need, and
  25|              distributed devices to students in households with no device or one device shared by all members of the
  26|              household. We worked with the IT Department, administrators, and teachers to establish norms and
  27|              expectations for staff, students, and families in a virtual classroom, as well as establishing a robust
  28|              schedule of instructional technology professional development offerings to prepare our teachers for a
  29|              transition from a virtual Enrichment Learning model to a Remote Learning model.
  30|              Before the 2019-2020 school-year came to a close, we were already preparing for a very different type of
  31|              school-year in 2020-2021. A Reopen Planning Team, comprised of thirty-five district and school
  32|              administrators, faculty, staff, parents, and transportation representatives was formed June 1, 2020. By June
  33|              30, 2020 members of this Team were assigned to Task Groups: Teaching and Learning; Healthy and Safe
  34|              Facilities; Behavioral Health; Special Services; Extended Programming; Human Resources and Operations;
  35|              and Equity, Access, and Engagement.
  36|                                                                                              103

===PAGE 104=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|              These Task Groups created recommendations which ultimately became the Fall 2020 Reopen Plan which was
   2|              presented to the Lunenburg School Committee on August 5, 2020.
   3|              The Reopen Plan, as per the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education, included an outline of three
   4|              different learning models: full Remote Learning, Hybrid Learning, full Return to Campus Learning.
   5|              Ultimately, due to the challenges of social distancing, the full Return to Campus was not the recommended
   6|              model for reopening. The full Remote Learning model was not recommended due to the strong need to have
   7|              our students get back to face-to-face learning. The Reopen Planning Team recommended and the School
   8|              Committee approved a Hybrid Learning model for reopening in Fall 2020. While the district reopened in the
   9|              Hybrid Learning model, families did have the option of remaining in a full Remote Learning model.

===PAGE 105=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|              Hybrid students were assigned to Cohorts. All students learn remotely on Mondays. Cohort A students
   2|              attend classes on campus on Tuesdays and Thursdays and learn remotely on Wednesdays and Fridays.
   3|              Cohort B students attend classes on Wednesdays and Fridays and learn remotely on Tuesdays and Thursdays.
   4|              By using Cohorts, the number of students on campus is reduced, allowing for implementation of the CDC
   5|              recommended six feet of social distancing. Families were asked to register for bus transportation as numbers
   6|              of students on a bus was also limited due to social distancing requirements. In order to better meet the needs
   7|              of students on their hybrid remote day, at the start of Quarter 3 the Hybrid Learning model was enhanced
   8|              with the addition of livestreaming on hybrid remote learning days.
   9|              In order for the district to begin livestreaming, instructional technology required an upgrade. Funded through
  10|              COVID relief funds, teachers received an upgraded laptop with docking station, microphone, 27 inch
  11|              monitor, and stylus pen. The 2020-2021 school calendar was amended to include eight early release days.
  12|              On these days, students learn on campus in the morning on their Cohort day, are dismissed and transported
  13|               home, and continue learning remotely for the afternoon. Teachers and staff work together in the afternoons
  14|              to prepare and plan for livestreaming lessons, share best practices, or attend instructional technology
  15|              workshops.
  16|              Due to the closure of schools in the spring of 2020, MCAS assessments were not administered. As a result,
  17|              we do not have MCAS data to include in our annual report. As it was known that MCAS data would not be
  18|              available, the district procured online assessment platforms. These standardized assessments were
  19|              administered in the fall and winter of 2020. These assessments provided valuable information that allowed
  20|              for targeted intervention for students. These data also allow us to determine if our students are working on
  21|              grade-level. District, school, and educator evaluation goals for faculty and administrators focus on
  22|              effectively using new tools for teaching and new assessment tools to determine student performance and
  23|              growth.
  24|              Much of our normal practice required adjustment due to COVID safety protocols, and our professional
  25|               development practice required adjustment as well. We were able to continue our inter-district collaboration
  26|              with North Middlesex Regional School District by holding a virtual conference in November 2020. The
  27|              Lunenburg Professional Development Committee is excited to plan a regional professional development
  28|               conference in November 2021 and serve as a host campus.
  29|              Through grant funds the district was able to restore a director-level curriculum position which had been cut
  30|              in 2012. This position is essential to provide supervision of and assistance to teachers in a review of the
  31|              curriculum. Due to the school-year being reduced from 180 days to 170 days due to COVID-19, adjustments
  32|              to pacing, prioritization, and sequencing of the curriculum were necessary.
  33|                                                                                              105

===PAGE 106=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|               As MCAS was not administered in spring 2020 due to school closure, the district procured an online
   2|               standardized assessment tool in order to have the necessary data to measure student growth and
   3|               performance, and to determine which students require intervention to close learning gaps. Robust teacher
   4|               training in instructional and assessment technologies and sharing of best practice was built into the school
   5|               calendar. The Interim Director of Teaching and Learning and Title I Coordinator positions were integral to
   6|               all of this work.
   7|               I want to acknowledge and thank our staff for their professionalism and flexibility as we have continued to
   8|               make adjustments in order to provide the best learning experience possible for our students; our parents and
   9|               families for the sacrifices they have made in order to support their students' learning from home; and the
  10|               School Committee, Town Manager, and local boards and committees for their support of the schools
  11|               throughout the pandemic. Lunenburg has been successful in keeping our schools open and remaining
  12|               focused on improving the ways in which we support our students academically, socially, and emotionally
  13|               because of the strong partnership the schools have experienced with parents, extended families, and the
  14|               greater community.
  15|               "The oak fought the wind and was broken, the willow bent when it must and survived."
  16|               - Robert Jordan
  17|               For me, the highlight of this report annually is announcing the recipient of the Superintendent's Award.
  18|               The selection process is difficult as we have many hard working, high-performing, community service-
  19|               oriented students. The students this year are even more impressive, continuing to demonstrate their
  20|               resilience as COVID-19 has impacted the normalcy of life in so many ways. The recipient of the 2021
  21|               Superintendent's Award is Olivia Catalini. Olivia has certainly persevered, citing her work ethic and
  22|               disciplined nature as two qualities that have assisted her during high school and the pandemic.
  23|               Academically, she has achieved several honors and awards for excellence in Math, Science, History, Latin
  24|               and the Arts. Her skills of organization and attention to detail have been two key elements that have kept
  25|               her focused through seven AP courses taken.
  26|               Her curiosity and creativity has led her to pursue instrumental music in the form of guitar, as well as
  27|               various art forms such as watercolor, colored pencil and sketching.
  28|                Being a natural leader, she has helped coach her varsity tennis teammates on a co-op team between
  29|               Lunenburg and Fitchburg. Her many interests show her innovative side in photography, gaming and
  30|               graphic design which may lead her down an artistic path in her future.
  31|               In her own words, "I want to integrate into a college community which will help me grow as a person and
  32|               that I can give back in return." We wish Olivia all of the best as she pursues her future endeavors.
  33|                                                                                              106

===PAGE 107=== (ocr, 51 lines)
   1|                                                                                    School Facilities
   2|                                                                                         2020-2021
   3|               The focus of school maintenance for FY21 was to support operating the school in a COVID 19 environment,
   4|               as well preventative maintenance on heating and ventilation systems and energy conservation measures. The
   5|               focus of repairs continues to be on Turkey Hill Elementary School with a major focus on ensuring the
   6|               heating and ventilation system is fully operational.
   7|               COVID 19
   8|               The district prepared for opening during the epidemic by installing partitions in the school offices,
   9|               upgrading custodial equipment for disinfection, preparing supplies for the teaching environment such as
  10|               additional disinfectant spray bottles and microfiber wipers and changing cleaning protocols to add
  11|               additional disinfection periods during and after the school day. A close inspection was done of the
  12|               ventilation system at Turkey Hill and numerous repairs were completed prior to the opening of school.
  13|               Indoor Air Quality assessments were completed by using school personnel and confirmed that the
  14|               ventilation system is performing well against its original design.
  15|               Security Upgrades
  16|               The district applied for a Safer Schools and Community Grant in March 2020 and was awarded $50,000 to
  17|              install a Bi-Directional Antenna at Lunenburg Primary School for police and fire services. The project
  18|               completed by Cybercomm and Di-Rock Electric. Work began in July 2020 and was completed by the end of
  19|               August. The bi-directional antenna improves communication for both police and fire personnel while they
  20|               are in the building and ensure good communications back to the Public Safety Building.
  21|               Water Testing
  22|               In the third year of wide spread water testing for lead and copper in the drinking water at all schools in June
  23|               2020, lead was detected in a number of sources at Turkey Hill and Passios Elementary School. The source
  24|               of the lead is believed to be solder from joining copper pipes. The teaching staff, parents and students have
  25|               been made aware of the need to flush water sources at the start of the school day. A fixture replacement
  26|               program was begun for the Turkey Hill Elementary School to replace fixtures as well as closing in piping
  27|               for selected areas. Follow up testing will occur at all schools in June 2021.
  28|               Energy Savings
  29|               A Green Community Grant was awarded to the Town of Lunenburg to upgrade three appliances at Turkey
  30|               Hill Elementary School with Energy Star Appliances (two-door reach in refrigerator, milk cooler and a with Energy Star Appli
  31|               double convection oven) at a cost of $15,092. A LED lighting conversion for Lunenburg Middle High
  32|               School kitchen was begun in December 2020 and is expected to be complete in Feb 2021 with $2, 780.12 in
  33|               Green Community Grant tunas. A replacement domestic hot water heater for Lunenburg Primary School
  34|               was sent out for bid in January 2021 and is expected to be complete by April 2021 with $9,900 in green
  35|               community grant funds.
  36|               Capital Program
  37|               The FY 21 capital program continued to fund additional asbestos abatement at Turkey Hill Elementary
  38|               School. In July 2020, Encore Contracting Services for $93,836.25 removed spray-on fire proofing above the
  39|               ceiling in room 102, 113 and 114. This project included removal of the splined ceiling and the floor tiles in
  40|               those areas. This project was the final phase at Turkey Hill Elementary School to abate the spray-on
  41|               asbestos. The school continues to have vinyl floor tiles with asbestos based mastic in numerous areas.
  42|               Additional removal are in the capital plan for 4 years from now.
  43|               The school department also received funding to begin air conditioning design for Lunenburg Primary School
  44|               classroom wing. This work was awarded to DiGiorgio Associates for $10,000.
  45|               At the Special Town Meeting during November 2020, funding for split air conditioning systems was
  46|               approved at $28,000 to provide air conditioning for the main office, the guidance suite, and nurse's office.
  47|               This project is expected to be out to bid in February 2021 with work to begin around April 2021.
  48|               Respectfully submitted,
  49|               John Londa
  50|               Director of Facilities and Grounds
  51|                                                                                              107

===PAGE 108=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                                              Technology Department
   2|                                                                                         2020-2021
   3|                 Technology touches all our lives every day at the Lunenburg Public Schools. It is our aim in IT to serve our students, faculty and
   4|                staff proactively, so that the tools used to facilitate learning function seamlessly and responsively when
   5|                needed. From checking email, to accessing class information, to connecting and collaborating with students
   6|                and colleagues near and far, faculty, staff, and IT are working together to help redefine what is possible for
   7|                our students, so they can redefine what's possible for the world.
   8|                As we reflect on this last year, to say 2020 has been challenging would be an understatement. With the
   9|                arrival of the coronavirus in the spring, all our IT priorities shifted to an intense focus on responding to this
  10|                emergency and how best to support the district moving to remote teaching, learning and working almost
  11|                overnight while still maintaining our core services. This report provides information on the Technology
  12|                 Team, as well as some highlights of the work we have done in response to COVID-19.
  13|                The IT Team
  14|                Our team continues to learn and grow each year as we improve our infrastructure and explore innovations
  15|                in education technology.
  16|                Steve Malandrinos - Information Technology Director
  17|                      Eight years working for the Town and Schools
  18|                      Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA and
  19|                           Belchertown Public Schools
  20|                Jordan Pochini - School Technology Coordinator
  21|                • Six years working for the Town and Schools
  22|                • BS in Computer Science from Fitchburg State University
  23|                      Prior experience includes both individual contributor and management roles in retail environments
  24|                Heather Buiwit - Technology Assistant
  25|                      MBA in General Business from Nichols College, Dudley, MA
  26|                      Previously worked for the Lunenburg Public Library assisting with technology
  27|                Transitioning to remote teaching, learning and working required not just a shift in practice, but new
  28|                equipment and technology infrastructure to support a completely new model of instruction. While we were
  29|                able to provide devices to those students most in need for the end of the 2019-2020 school year, additional
  30|                student devices and updated teacher devices were needed to move effectively into the remote model -
  31|                especially to support synchronous teaching or 'live streaming' of classes. Drawing on grant funding
  32|                sources, as well as local funds where needed, several large technology purchases were placed throughout
  33|                the year.
  34|                Over the summer, orders of Chromebooks and iPads were placed in sufficient quantity to put a device in
  35|                the hands of every Lunenburg student. Configuration and deployment was definitely a challenge for our
  36|                small team, but with help from district and building administration, we were able to set up outdoor pick-up
  37|                sites to safely put the devices into families' hands.
  38|                Later in the year, when it became clear that the live streaming model of teaching would be adopted, grant
  39|                funding was used to purchase updated laptops, monitors, and additional peripherals for teachers to facilitate
  40|                this new mode of instruction.
  41|                                                                                              108

===PAGE 109=== (ocr, 45 lines)
   1|              A demo classroom was set up at each school, and training sessions were held over the course of a few weeks
   2|              to give teachers a chance to see the equipment in action and discuss scenarios for effectively employing it.
   3|              Training sessions were well attended and lively, often resulting in classroom visits to experiment with
   4|              different configurations and answer individual questions.
   5|              To support the new network traffic associated with live stream video conferencing, upgraded internet
   6|              connections were needed at Lunenburg Primary School and Turkey Hill Elementary School. Fiber optic
   7|              lines were run to each building, and new service came online mid-January, increasing bandwidth
   8|              from100Mbps download, 20Mbps upload to 300Mbps download and upload.
   9|              We don't know exactly what the future will hold for 2021, but there are signs of hope with vaccines and
  10|              outdoor weather on the way. One thing I do know is that the way we work and teach will forever be changed
  11|              by this experience, and technology is the key to supporting this shift. Lunenburg IT continues to adjust its
  12|              priorities to do all we can to support the district's evolving academic plans, and to support teaching and
  13|              learning as we navigate these uncharted waters.
  14|              Respectfully Submitted,
  15|              Steve Malandrinos, IT Director
  16|                                                                         Special Services Department
  17|                                                             FY20 (data taken from 2019-2020 school year)
  18|              From the March 1, 2020 report (SIMS Report 5-Enrollment Statistics - District Summary), there are 257
  19|              students with disabilities receiving special education or related services in Lunenburg (last year there were
  20|              263). This represents 15.5% of our total student population of 1,659 students (it was 15.8% last year). The
  21|              state average from the 2019-20 Selected Populations Report (https://profiles.doe.mass.edu/statereport/
  22|              selectedpopulations.aspx) is 18.4. From the March 1, 2020 report (SIMS Report 7-SPED Enrollment
  23|              Statistics - District Summary), the total number of students receiving services outside the district was 11
  24|              (last year was 12). Of the 11 students, 3 are placed in Collaborative Placements, 3 are in Day Placements
  25|              and 5 are in Residential Placements. Our Advanced Community Experience Program (ACE), which serves
  26|              students ages 18-22years old, had 3 students enrolled last year. Additionally, from the March 1, 2020 report,
  27|              111 students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg (last year was
  28|              138).
  29|              For the 2020 school year, our overall classification is NOT REQUIRING ASSISTANCE OR
  30|              INTERVENTION as we are making substantial progress toward targets. Our progress toward improvement
  31|             targets is listed at 55% - substantial progress toward targets. The data below is from last school year as the
  32|              Massachusetts Department of Elementary and Secondary Education did not issue school, district, or state
  33|              accountability determinations for the 2019-20 school year due to the cancellation of state assessments and
  34|              school closures related to COVID-19.
  35|                         For the 2019 school year, as a district, our overall Accountability and Assistance Level is NOT
  36|              REQUIRING ASSISTANCE OR INTERVENTION with the Primary School (Insufficient data), Turkey Hill
  37|              Elementary School (Not requiring assistance or intervention), Lunenburg Middle School (Not requiring
  38|              assistance or intervention), Lunenburg High School (Not requiring assistance or intervention) and the
  39|              Advanced Community Experience Program (Insufficient data). The 2019 Official Accountability Report
  40|              classifies "High Needs" students in Lunenburg as "Moderate progress towards targets" in their overall
  41|              progress toward improvement targets. Participation rates in MCAS this year were as follows: High Needs =
  42|              99%, Econ. Disadvantaged = 99%, EL and Former EL = 100%, and Students w/disabilities = 100%.

   1|              Indicator 5 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 6-21 with IEPs. For 2018-2019, the state's rates

   6|              Indicator 6 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 3-5 with IEPs. The 2018-2019 state rates showed
   7|              the percent of students receiving a majority of their special education and related services in a Full Inclusion
   8|              early childhood program was 54.8%, in a Partial Inclusion Program was 18.6%, a Substantially Separate
   9|              Placement (special education class, separate school, or residential facility) was 16.2%, and students not
  10|              attending an early childhood program but receiving only a related service (e.g. speech, OT, PT) was
  11|              10.5%. In Lunenburg, our early childhood Full Inclusion rate was 34.0%, our Partial Inclusion rate was
  12|              15.1%, Substantially Separate rate was 26.4%, and Related Services was 24.5%.
  13|              Indicator 7 - Early Childhood Outcomes. Districts participate in Indicator 7 data collection as part of a 4
  14|              year cycle in which they report exit data on children for whom they have collected entry level data; exit may
  15|              occur at any time during the subsequent three year period as the children leave the preschool
  16|              program. Lunenburg did not have a cohort completion year in 2018-2019.
  17|              Indicator 8 - Parent Involvement. This indicator measures the % of parents with a child receiving special
  18|              education who report that school facilitated parent involvement as a means of improving services and results
  19|              for students with IEPs. For 2018-2019 the state target was 86.5%. Lunenburg's rate was 57.1% and the
  20|              state's rate was 60.1% (from information collected in the spring of 2019).
  21|              Indicator 9 & 10 - Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and in Special
  22|              Education Categories. In Lunenburg, the state determined that there was no disproportionality found in
  23|              either case.
  24|              Indicator 11 - Initial Evaluation Timelines. This indicator measures the % of children with parental consent
  25|              to evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established timeline. Data is reported for all initial
  26|              evaluations initiated in October, November, and December of the reporting year. Lunenburg was rated 90%
  27|              in compliance and the state average for this Indicator was 100%.
  28|              Indicator 12 - Early Childhood Transition. This indicator measures the % of children referred by Part C,
  29|              found eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by their third
  30|              birthdays. The state target for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is noted as "Suppressed". Data is
  31|              suppressed if the number of students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is
  32|              fewer than 10.
  33|              Indicator 13 - Secondary Transition. In all years, the state target for Secondary Transition is 100%. This
  34|              indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with an IEP that includes appropriate
  35|              measurable postsecondary goals that are annually updated and based upon an age appropriate transition
  36|              assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that will reasonably enable the student to meet
  37|              those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related to the student's transition services needs. Lunenburg
  38|              was last reviewed in this area in 2017-2018 and was 83.3% in compliance.
  39|              Indicator 14 - Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs. This indicator measures the % of students with
  40|              IEPs who exited high school during the 2017-2018 school year and self-reported post-school engagement in
  41|              education or employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement is defined through three
  42|              measurements:
  43|              • Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  44|                                                                                              111

===PAGE 112=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|             • Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high
   2|                   school.
   3|             • Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training
   4|                   program; or competitively employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high
   5|                   school.
   6|             Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post-
   7|             secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2018-2019 school year, state targets for the
   8|             three measurements of Post-School Outcomes are Measurement A: 50.2%; Measurement B: 79.6%; and
   9|             Measurement C: 87.3%. Lunenburg rates are as follows (from reporting year 2018-19): Measurement A =
  10|             50%, Measurement B = 75%, and Measurement C = 75%.
  11|                        The Parent Advisory Council Supporting All Learners (PACSAL) continues to be an active support
  12|             group for parents, teachers and administration. All parents and community members can join the group,
  13|             which currently consists of parents of students receiving Title I services and parents of students with
  14|             disabilities. Meetings are typically held the first Friday of each month, 11:00AM, in the Planning Room at
  15|             the Middle School (for the 2020-2021 school year, meetings were held via Zoom due to Covid-19). The
  16|             executive board is made up of Co-Chairpersons: Kelly Harvey and Liz Otero. Several events and
  17|             presentations were organized by the PACSAL for both parents and teachers in Lunenburg this year. For more
  18|             information, go to:
  19|                        https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/lps/special-services/pacsal?authuser=0
  20|                        In addition to the district PACSAL, Lunenburg also participates in a Regional SEPAC group.
  21|             Information from this group is shared on Facebook at "Regional SEPAC - Central MA"
  22|                        The Lunenburg Public Schools continues to provide a variety of special needs services to meet the
  23|             varying needs of our students in the least restrictive environment. We have an experienced, hard working staff
  24|             that works together with parents to provide these varied services. Our parents are equal partners in our
  25|             efforts to develop challenging and focused educational programs for children. We remain committed to the
  26|             Lunenburg Public Schools mission, "The mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students
  27|             with the skills, confidence, and passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to
  28|             successful careers, active citizenship, and rewarding lives." This mission, we feel, was even able to be upheld
  29|             during the unfortunate school closings due to the pandemic this year from March - June 2020. During this
  30|              time, special education services were still able to be delivered to students remotely and all DESE
  31|             recommendations and protocols were put into place and followed in order to meet the needs of IEPs as best as
  32|             possible. In addition, the Special Services Office also created a specific parent centered page called "Covid-
  33|             19 Information" that worked to help keep parents as updated as possible on all the current pandemic concerns
  34|             related to students with disabilities
  35|                        https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/Ips/special-services/covid-19-information?authuser-0
  36|                        We are proud of our staff, students, and families and their resiliency during this time and how they
  37|             came together to work under very stressful times. For additional information, please visit our website at:
  38|                                        https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/Ips/special-services

===PAGE 113=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                                           Teaching & Learning Models and PD Update
   2|                                                                                       2020-2021
   3|              Lunenburg Public School's Instructional Leadership Team and the Reopening Team's Teaching and
   4|              Learning Task Group developed multiple teaching and learning models for the 2020-2021 school year.
   5|              Upon return to school in the fall, students had a choice to return to a hybrid model of learning or remain in
   6|              a fully remote learning model.
   7|              Hybrid & Remote Learning Models
   8|              In our hybrid learning model, approximately 50% of students in the model attend school in person on
   9|              Tuesdays and Thursdays while learning remotely the other days of the week, and the other 50% attend in
  10|              person on Wednesdays and Fridays while learning remotely the other days of the week. High needs
  11|              students are prioritized for in-person learning four days per week. Mondays are a remote learning day for
  12|              all students during which students meet virtually with teachers during a portion of the day and complete
  13|              independent work for the remainder. This allows for classroom configurations to be altered for social
  14|              distancing.
  15|              A percentage of Lunenburg students chose to remain in a fully remote learning model. Specific teachers at
  16|              each grade level have been assigned to fully remote classes which students attend virtually through Google
  17|              Meet and which include a combination of synchronous and asynchronous learning experiences. At
  18|              Lunenburg High School, some remote students are enrolled in select virtual education classes through
  19|              Educere.
  20|              All students in the district were provided with 1:1 devices, Chromebooks for students in grades 3-12 and
  21|              iPads for students in PreK-2.
  22|              Our teachers have worked diligently to create welcoming physical and virtual classrooms and to provide
  23|              robust and rigorous learning and social emotional experiences for students in an environment where
  24|              digitizing and reimagining curriculum is essential but extremely challenging. All teachers use Google
  25|              Classroom as a learning management system in order to post assignments, announcements, instructions,
  26|              videos, presentations, links to virtual meetings through Google Meet, and other instructional materials and
  27|              tools. Some of the instructional and assessment platforms employed by teachers include district-
  28|              purchased subscriptions to iReady, Lexia, IXL, Newsela, Brainpop, Raz-Kids, and Nearpod. In addition,
  29|              teachers are using Google Workplace elements such as Docs, Slides, Sheets, Sites, Forms, and Jamboard to
  30|              design and implement lessons in addition to other tools and resources such as Khan Academy, EdPuzzle,
  31|              Kahoot, CommonLit, Gimkit, and Flipgrid to name a few. Many teachers have also recorded lessons or
  32|              read alouds with Meet and Screencastify to provide students with additional personalized instruction.
  33|              Collaboration has been essential for this dramatic shift in teaching as teams of teachers consistently work
  34|              together to create materials, plan lessons, engage students, and design curriculum for optimum learning
  35|              outcomes for students.
  36|              Live streaming
  37|              In January 2021, our hybrid model changed to incorporate live streaming so that students in the remote
  38|              cohort on Tuesday-Thursday join the in-person cohort for class as one community. Live streaming was
  39|              piloted by select high school classes during the fall and by our 8th grade teachers in December, 2020, and
  40|              best practices were shared with colleagues during professional development and multiple question and
  41|              answer sessions. In preparation for live streaming throughout the district, teachers were provided with new
  42|              laptops, a second monitor, microphones/speakers, docking stations, and tablets.
  43|                                                                                              113

===PAGE 114=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|              Students now experience synchronous instruction and learning each school day as each building has
   2|              designed developmentally appropriate schedules. Already, students have gained live instruction time,
   3|              participated in whole-class discussions, and partnered with members of the opposite cohort on collaborative
   4|              documents and in breakout rooms. Teachers continue to refine and adjust practices in order to negotiate
   5|              technology, engage students, encourage collaboration and discussion, and strengthen learning.
   6|              Professional Development & Training
   7|              Our district has provided professional development and training sessions throughout the school year in order
   8|              to support teachers with these learning models and to prepare for live streaming. District staff participated
   9|              in professional development during the opening 10 days of school which included professional learning in
  10|              the areas of social emotional learning, preparing for hybrid and remote learning models, safety training,
  11|              literacy planning, creating a safe and supportive school environment, reviewing social justice standards, and
  12|              Response to Intervention. We also offered 20 choices of technology training sessions led by experienced
  13|              district staff, including sessions on Google Classroom and Meet, interactive notebooks, EdPuzzle, Google
  14|              Sites for student portfolios, Nearpod, choice boards, bitmoji classrooms, using document cameras, and
  15|              iMovie. Formal training on purchased instructional platforms, iReady and IXL, was also offered to the
  16|              district.
  17|               A cross-district professional development day was held on November 3rd in collaboration with North
  18|              Middlesex Regional School District. Our keynote speaker, Lynn Lyons, presented, "Teaching in a Time of
  19|              Uncertainty: The Emotional Skills Our Kids Need and the Patterns to Target." Lynn also offered secondary
  20|              and elementary seminars on approaches to anxiety, worry, and depression. A variety of professional
  21|              learning sessions were offered including multiple technology sessions, special interest groups, and sessions
  22|              on educator self-care, mindfulness, engagement strategies, fostering mentoring relationships, best teaching
  23|              practices, supporting transgender and binary students, creating virtual bookrooms, and trauma response.
  24|              Staff had the choice as to which learning best suited their needs.
  25|              In preparation for the shift to live streaming, we held four half PD days during which tech demo rooms were
  26|              available for teachers in each building and live streaming training was offered in addition to tech tools and
  27|              Q&A sessions.
  28|              Data sessions with iReady have been ongoing this year, and PD for responding to data, reading and writing
  29|              workshops, phonics, and phonological awareness will also continue.
  30|              Our Professional Development Committee will proceed to check-in with the district to evaluate needs and
  31|              prepare for upcoming professional learning including future book studies and workshops.
  32|              Respectfully Submitted,
  33|              Annica Scott
  34|              Interim Director of Teaching & Learning
  35|                                                                                              114

===PAGE 115=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                                                                          Title I and Literacy Update
   2|                                                                                        2020-2021
   3|               A comprehensive assessment of Title I and Literacy in grades K-5 conducted in July 2020 revealed many
   4|              strengths and some areas for improvement particularly in light of new considerations caused by the
   5|              pandemic. The need for clarity and communication of standards that needed reinforcement at the start of
   6|              the new school year; a well articulated and communicated scope and sequence of standards for the 2020-
   7|              2021 school year; reliable, vertically aligned assessments that provide real-time and actionable information
   8|              for teachers; and print and digital resources to support the standards and student needs identified were the
   9|              key areas that educators focused on to assure robust implementation and learning for all students.
  10|              In the area of Title I services the following actions were taken and will continue to be a focus for deep
  11|              implementation throughout the remainder of the 2020-2021 school year.
  12|              • identify and set expectations for the use of resources (human and curricula) as additional support for
  13|                    identified students
  14|              • hire highly qualified Title I staff- 3 tutors to join a paraprofessional and Reading Specialist
  15|               • acquire and support the ettective use of an assessment system as a component of eligibility and to
  16|                    monitor student progress including PD provided by iReady to implement the system and respond to
  17|                    results through changes in instruction.
  18|              • ensure that all identified students have equitable access to instructional time with Title I staff through
  19|                    master scheduling and refreshed selection criteria informed by Title I federal guidelines
  20|              • acquire and support the consistent implementation of instructional resources for Title I intervention
  21|                    (Lul, IReady, Lexia)
  22|              • acquire and support the ettective use of an instructional plattorm tor Title 1 intervention (Ikeady, Lexia)
  23|              • provide training, coaching, modeling for Title I staff in pedagogy and programming-phonemic
  24|                    awareness, Leveled Literacy Intervention, Developmental Reading Inventory, iReady, Lexia
  25|              • Title I presentations delivered synchronously and asynchronously during curriculum nights, school
  26|                    committee meeting, SEPAC meeting, and School Council Meetings
  27|              In Literacy, the following actions were taken and will continue to be a focus for deep implementation
  28|              throughout the remainder of the 2020-2021 school year.
  29|              • concerted, collaborative effort to implement a cohesive reading and writing program with fidelity so that
  30|                    every child has access to core resources and pedagogy every day achieved through vertically articulated
  31|                    scope and sequence in reading and writing, continuous acquisition and evaluation of print and digital
  32|                    resources to support the scope and sequence, and collaborative grade level and vertical planning
  33|              • consistent use of assessments and collection and use of data to adjust and plan instruction and monitor
  34|                    student growth and achievement
  35|              • evidence based universal screeners through the acquisition of iReady diagnostic assessment to identify
  36|                    student needs, students who are potentially at risk for reading tallure, including those who may have
  37|                    developmental reading disabilities, and as a component for determining Title I eligibility.
  38|               Upcoming activities to support Title I and Literacy actions for continuous improvement
  39|              • Continued PD Grades K-5 for responding to data
  40|               •    Continued PD for grade 2 in reading and writing workshop
  41|              • Continued PD for grades K and 1 in phonics
  42|              • Implementation of early childhood grant with procurement of phonological awareness curriculum for
  43|                    grades PreK and K with professional development and coaching'
  44|              • Supporting family engagement with the relaunch of Footsteps to Brilliance application with PD for
  45|                    families in February and April and summer reading launch in June.
  46|               Respectfully submitted,
  47|               Rebecca Hyde, Title I Coordinator/Literacy Specialist

  15|               Sheryl Ewen
  16|               Eating a healthy breakfast before starting the school day is linked to improved concentration. Breakfast is
  17|               especially important for young students whose brains use up about half of the body's energy. Eating lunch is
  18|               important because it provides nourishment to the body and brain and reduces stress. Therefore, in the spring
  19|               of 2020 when the COVID-19 pandemic began, we knew we had to get meals to children somehow. We
  20|               quickly devised a plan, set up a curbside grab and go meal site. With the help of the nursing and central
  21|               office staff, we delivered meals to families that could not get to us. In April 2020, we could only provide
  22|               meals to families that qualified for meal benefits through the National School Lunch Program (NSLP). By
  23|               May 2020, the United States Department of Agricultures (USDA) Food and Nutrition Service (ENS)
  24|               approved a request by the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE) to allow school
  25|               food authorities flexibility while continuing to feed children. We applied for waivers and were able provide
  26|               meals to all children 18 and under at no cost to families but allowing our program reimbursement for each
  27|               meal served. As of December 23 2020, we have distributed 31,636 meals to children and teens 18 and
  28|                Dalia Marzuca, Zack Welton and Valerie Martin resigned from their position to pursue different options.
  29|                We wish them luck in their new endeavors.
  30|               Respectfully Submitted,
  31|               Nadine Lorenzen, Food Service Director

===PAGE 117=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                         LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL 2020-2021
   2|                          The Lunenburg Primary School is an early childhood education center for students in preschool to
   3|              second grade. Our students range from three years old to eight years old. Our school community consists of
   4|              students, teachers, parents and support staff working together to maintain a learning environment that
   5|              focuses on the academic and social emotional needs of the whole child.
   6|                          The October 1, 2020 student enrollment reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary
   7|              Education was 363 students. The following grade level class sizes reflect the adjustment to the Hybrid
   8|              reopening model following the guidelines from the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education
   9|              and the Center for Disease Control.
  10|                          Currently, our Preschool program services 36 three and four year old students in two classrooms
  11|              with multiple sessions. We have six Kindergarten classes servicing 106 students. Class sizes in our in-
  12|              person Kindergartens range from 14-16 and are broken into two cohorts of 7-9 students. The full remote
  13|              Kindergarten class has 33 students. There are four in person first grade classrooms with class sizes ranging
  14|              between 21-25 and are broken into two cohorts of 10-13 students. Our Full remote first grade classroom has
  15|              18 students. We have five in person second grade classrooms that serve 135 students with class sizes
  16|              averaging 22 -24 students broken into two cohorts of 10-14 students. Our full remote second grade
  17|              classroom has 31 students.
  18|              The COVID -19 Pandemic changed the context in which the Primary School provides instruction to the
  19|              student population that it serves. Consideration for the safety and welfare of students and staff became part
  20|              of our usual overall planning process. Guidelines for physical distancing, wearing masks and sanitizing
  21|              were at the forefront of any planning. Staff collaborated on developing protocols for classroom routines,
  22|              hallway transitions, arrival and dismissal procedures, changes to traditional recess practices, and the
  23|              distribution of needed instructional materials. Virtual meetings have become the norm for staff meetings,
  24|              common planning meetings, professional development remote cohort student instruction, IEP, 504, and
  25|              parent meetings. Communication to families and the community have also used virtual platforms. Parent
  26|              forums or surveys to solicit feedback on the school's performance have also become part of our routine.
  27|               Professional development continues to focus on literacy instruction this year. Working with the district's
  28|              Title I Coordinator we identified, and secured additional units of study from Lucy Calkins' Reading and
  29|              Writing Workshop model that would be beneficial for staff at all grade levels to continue their work with
  30|              regard to literacy instruction. The goal is to prepare students for any reading and writing task they will face
  31|              and to turn kids into life-long, confident readers and writers who display agency and independence.
  32|              Additional professional development focusing on technology, instructional software and their use in
  33|              instruction became a priority. Providing instructional opportunities for students in person and remotely
  34|              required additional technologies including, document cameras and additional monitors. Google Classroom
  35|              has taken the place of teacher webpages as the main platform providing information, assignments and
  36|              resources for students and their families. Instructional programs such as IXL, RAZ kids, Epic have become
  37|              additional tools for teachers to use to assist with student support and growth. We have adopted a new
  38|              diagnostic tool that is used to assess student growth throughout the year. The iReady diagnostic assessment
  39|              tool provides student performance and growth data. It provides an additional data point that is used to
  40|              provide instruction and support for students.
  41|                                                                                              117

===PAGE 118=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|              Building Culture and Climate continues to be of the utmost importance during this school year. The staff
   2|              has made great efforts to include traditional activities for students while being mindful of the necessary
   3|              safety protocols. Our staff works to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive.
   4|              Through the continued use of Positive Behavior Intervention and Supports (PBIS). Positive Behavioral
   5|              Interventions and Supports (PBIS) is an evidence-based three-tiered framework for improving and
   6|              integrating all of the data, systems, and practices affecting student outcomes every day. We have worked
   7|              on establishing and teaching the school wide expectations along with having a continuum of encouraging
   8|              expected behavior and discouraging problem behaviors. This system is aligning nicely with The Anchors of
   9|              Emotional Intelligence, a whole-school/district-wide approach to teaching social emotional learning, which
  10|              the staff has been trained in and is using to help students recognize, understand, describe, communicate and
  11|              regulate their emotions. These approaches have been supplemented by the creation of the Behavioral Health
  12|              Support Team (BHST) which provides tiered support strategies for students behavioral and mental health.
  13|               The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to ensure
  14|              that all students have the services they need to be successful. Common assessments are completed in the
  15|              fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These
  16|              common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and the progress of students.
  17|              Struggling students are identified for additional support in Reading, Math, Speech, Occupational and
  18|              Physical Therapy through our TST (Teacher Support Team) process.
  19|              The Primary School Advisory Council meets each month to discuss school related concerns. Serving as an
  20|              advisory group to the principal, the council is charged with reviewing the school building budget and
  21|              developing the school improvement plan.
  22|              Members of the School Advisory Council for the 2020-2021 school year are:
  23|              Principal:                                           Chad Adams
  24|              Assistant Principal                                  Kevin White
  25|              Teachers:                                            Sara Doyle, Kathryn Gerrior, Kara Slattery, Kirsten Snape
  26|              Parents:                                             Tiffaney Tavares, Shaunna Baril, Meghan Arnold
  27|              Community Representative:                            Vacant
  28|              School Committee Rep:                               Laura Kelly
  29|              I am honored and humbled to be afforded the opportunity to work with a highly dedicated staff and a caring
  30|              community in order to provide the best possible educational opportunities for our youngest students. For
  31|              more information about the work that we do at the Lunenburg Primary School please visit our website:
  32|              https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/primary/
  33|              Respectfully submitted,
  34|              Chad Adams, Principal
